Geometric obstructions for ξ \xi -fillings of 3-manifolds
Daniel Galvin, Peter Teichner, Simona Veselá

Abstract
. We consider the realisation problem for normal 1-types of 4-manifolds with a given boundary. More precisely, given a normal 1-type ξ \xi and closed 3-dimensional ξ \xi -manifold Y Y , does there exist a compact 4-dimensional ξ \xi -manifold with boundary Y Y ? We describe a three stage obstruction theory for the existence of such a 4-manifold, with our main contribution being a ‘tertiary’ obstruction that we describe geometrically via Wall’s quadratic self-intersection form.



1.
Introduction

An application of Kreck’s modified surgery theory
[
18
]
reduces the problem of classifying compact, smooth 4-manifolds up to
(S^{2}\times S^{2})
-stable diffeomorphism to computing certain
\xi
-bordism groups. Here
\xi:B\to BO
is the
normal 1-type
of the manifolds
X
in question, a Postnikov-Moore approximation of the stable normal bundle
\nu_{X}:X\to BO
. So
\xi
induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups
\pi_{i}
for
i>2
(and a monomorphism on
\pi_{2}
) and there is a lift
u_{X}:X\to B
of
\nu_{X}
that is an isomorphism on
\pi_{i}
for
i<2
(and an epimorphism on
\pi_{2}
).

We call
u_{X}
a
normal 1-smoothing
on
X
and whenever
u_{M}\colon M\to B
lifts the stable normal bundle of a manifold
M
over
\xi
, we call
(M,u_{M})
a
\xi
-manifold and
u_{M}
a
\xi
-structure. If
Y
is a closed 3-manifold with
\xi
-structure
u_{Y}
, one can ask the following existence and uniqueness questions:

(1)

Does
(Y,u_{Y})
have a
\xi
-filling, i.e. a 4-dimensional
\xi
-manifold
(X,u_{X})
with boundary
(Y,u_{Y})
?

(2)

Can we choose a
\xi
-filling
(X,u_{X})
to be a normal 1-smoothing?

(3)

How many such 1-smoothings
(X,u_{X})
exist up to stable diffeomorphism?

By doing surgeries in the interior of
X

\xi
-fillings can be turned into normal 1-smoothings (
Lemma

2.7
), answering (2) modulo (1). Kreck’s theorem turns (3) into a question about 5-dimensional
\xi
-fillings, as follows. Two normal 1-smoothings
(X_{i},u_{i})
with boundary
(Y,u_{Y})
are stably diffeomorphic (with boundary diffeomorphism
\varphi:Y\to Y
) if and only if
X_{1}\cup_{\varphi}-X_{2}
admits a 5-dimensional
\xi
-filling.
As a consequence, (1) is a lower dimensional cousin of the stable classification question (3), addressing the
existence
question for normal 1-smoothings
(X,u_{X})
with given boundary condition. It is the purpose of this paper.

We start with a
\xi
-manifold
(Y,u_{Y})
as above and ask whether it represents zero in the bordism group
\Omega_{3}^{\xi}
of
\xi
-manifolds. One case is very easy, namely when
\pi_{2}(B)\neq 0
(the
totally non-spin
case) and hence
\pi_{2}(\xi)
gives an isomorphism
\pi_{2}(B)\cong\pi_{2}(BO)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
. Then
B
is homotopy equivalent to
BSO\times B\pi
and
\Omega_{3}^{\xi}\cong H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})
. Here
\pi:=\pi_{1}(B)
and
w_{1}^{\pi}\in H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the first Stiefel-Whitney class of
\xi
restricted to
B\pi
. In this case,
(Y,u_{Y})
has a
\xi
-filling if and only if
(p_{2}\circ u_{Y})_{*}[Y]=0\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})
, giving a complete answer to (1).

From now on we assume that
\pi_{2}(B)=0
, so the normal 1-type
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})\colon B\to BO
is the pullback of the universal Stiefel-Whitney classes
(w_{1},w_{2}):BO\to B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
and
(w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi}):B\pi\to B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
. In particular,
there is a fibration
p:B\to B\pi
such that the restriction of
\xi
to a fibre
BSpin
is the canonical projection.

In this situation, the second-named author
[
27
]
(cf.
Theorem

B.7
) constructed what he called the “James spectral sequence” for
\xi
. The skeleta of
B\pi
induce a filtration

\{0\}=F_{0,3}\leq F_{1,2}\leq F_{2,1}\leq F_{3,0}=\Omega_{3}^{\xi},

for which we will give new geometric interpretations.

Any
\xi
-structure on
M
gives
p\circ u_{M}\colon M\to B\pi
over
w_{1}^{\pi}
. If
(Y,p\circ u_{Y})
bounds a compact 4-manifold
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
with
u_{X}^{\pi}\colon X\to B\pi
an extension of
p\circ u_{Y}
then we call it a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
.
There is a single obstruction
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{X}^{\pi},u_{Y})\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, to extending the
\xi
-structure
u_{Y}
over
X
, relative to
u_{X}^{\pi}
.

Theorem A
.

Let
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})\colon B\to BO
be a normal 1-type with
\pi_{2}(B)=0
and let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a 3-dimensional
\xi
-manifold. For the above filtration of
\Omega_{3}^{\xi}
the corresponding geometric conditions are as follows:

(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{2,1}

\Longleftrightarrow

There exists a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
.

(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{1,2}

\Longleftrightarrow

There exists a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
of
(Y,u_{Y})
, such that
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{X}^{\pi},u_{Y})
has a spherical representative.

(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{0,3}

\Longleftrightarrow

There exists a
\xi
-filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
. Moreover,

(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{2,1}\Longleftrightarrow

(p\circ u_{Y})_{*}[Y]=0\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})
.

If
(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{2,1}
, then
(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{1,2}\Longleftrightarrow

(u_{X}^{\pi})_{*}\PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{X}^{\pi},u_{Y}))=0\in H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/(\delta_{2})
.

If
(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{1,2}
, then
(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{0,3}\Longleftrightarrow

For a spherical representative
c
of
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{X}^{\pi},u_{Y})
we have
[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)]=0\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/(\delta_{2},\delta_{3})
.

where
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
denotes a certain modification of Wall’s self-intersection number for spheres, which we introduce in
Section

3
, and
\delta_{2}
and
\delta_{3}
correspond to certain ‘geometric differentials’ which we will define later.

Wall’s quadratic self intersection form
\mu\colon\pi_{2}(X)\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]/(\sim)
counts self-intersections (with fundamental group elements) of immersed spheres in a 4-manifold
X
, and is valued in a certain quotient of the group ring
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
(which we will make precise in
Section

3
). We define an adjustment of this
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}\colon\pi_{2}(X)\to I/(\sim)
, where
I:=\ker(\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\to\mathbb{Z})
denotes the augmentation ideal. We then define
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
by postcomposing with a projection map
I/(\sim)\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, which we will describe in
Section

3
. To define the tertiary invariant in terms of the self-intersection of the class
c
(as in the statement of
A
) we need to make it not depend on various choices made therein. This quotient is in terms of certain ‘geometric differentials’
\delta_{*}
that we now describe briefly. They will mirror the differentials in the James spectral sequence, which we denote by
d_{*}
. We define the first as

\displaystyle\delta^{(4,0)}_{2}\colon H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})

\displaystyle\to H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)

\displaystyle(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}[M]

\displaystyle\mapsto(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}\PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,\emptyset,u_{M}^{\pi},\emptyset))

where
(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
is a closed 4-manifold representing
a
(implicitly this means that the image of the obstruction does not depend on this choice). Again, let
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
be a filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
such that
u_{X}^{\pi}
induces an isomorphism on fundamental groups (this can be achieved by surgery). Then there is the following exact sequence corresponding to the fibration
u_{X}^{\pi}
(see
Appendix

A
).

(1.1)

H_{3}(X;Z/2)\xrightarrow{(u_{X}^{\pi})_{*}}H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}}\pi_{2}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{h_{2}^{\Lambda}}H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\xrightarrow{(u_{X}^{\pi})_{*}}H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)

Hence, for
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, choosing a lift for
\mathfrak{f}(z)
produces an immersed sphere which is null-
\mathbb{Z}/2
-homologous (in fact, all such spheres are produced in this way). We define the second geometric differential as

\displaystyle\delta^{(3,1)}_{2}\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)

\displaystyle\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(c,w)

where
w
is an immersed sphere representative for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X^{\prime},Y,u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi},u_{Y})
,
c
denotes a representative immersed sphere for
\mathfrak{f}(z)
, and
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}
denotes a suitable normalisation of the equivariant intersection form on
X
(in line with how
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
is defined). We show this is well-defined in
Section

5.2
.

Theorem B
.

Let
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal 1-type. Then
\delta^{(4,0)}_{2}=d_{2}^{(4,0)}
, where
d_{2}^{(4,0)}
is the corresponding differential in the James spectral sequence, and

\delta^{(3,1)}_{2}=d_{2}^{(3,1)}
, where
d_{2}^{(3,1)}
is the corresponding differential in the James spectral sequence.

The geometric differential
\delta_{3}^{(4,0)}
is defined as

\displaystyle\delta_{3}^{(4,0)}\colon\ker(\delta_{2}^{(4,0)})\to

\displaystyle H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/(\delta_{2}^{(3,1)})

\displaystyle(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}[M]\mapsto

\displaystyle\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{J}(c_{M})

where
(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
is a closed 4-manifold with
\delta_{3}^{(4,0)}(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}[M])=0
and
c_{M}\in\pi_{2}(M)
is a spherical representative of
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
. We show that this is well-defined in
Section

5.3
.

Remark 1.1
.

It is a consequence of
A
that the image of
\delta_{3}^{(4,0)}
coincides with the image of
d_{3}^{(4,0)}
but we do not know if they coincide as maps. This fact is stated later as
Proposition

6.11
.

A well-known theorem due to Wu (see e.g.
[
20
, Theorem 11.14]
) applied to 4-manifolds gives the formula

Q(\alpha,w)=Q(\alpha,\alpha)\mod{2},

where
Q
denotes the intersection form on homology of a closed 4-manifold
X
,
w
denotes the Poincaré dual of the second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}(X)
, and
\alpha\in H_{2}(X)
. A corollary of
B
is the following secondary version of this Wu formula, which may be of independent interest.

Theorem C
.

[
Secondary Wu formula
]

Let
X
be a compact 4-manifold with boundary
Y
. For any choice of normal spin structure
s
on
Y
, let
w_{2}^{s}\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)
denote the relative second Stiefel-Whitney class. Let
c
be an immersed spherical representative for
w_{2}^{s}
, and let
b
be any null-
\mathbb{Z}/2
-homologous immersed sphere in
X
. Fix any map
u_{X}^{\pi}\colon X\to B\pi
. Then in
I
we have

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(b,c)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{J}(b)\mod{IJ}

where
I
and
J
denote the
\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Z}/2
augmentation ideals of
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(X)]
, respectively and
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}
,
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}
are defined in Section
3
.

Interestingly,
C
applies outside of the relative setting that the rest of the paper is in.

Remark 1.2
.

All of the data that is required to state the Secondary Wu formula can be obtained from the
quadratic 2-type

(\pi_{1}(X),\pi_{2}(X),k(X),\lambda(X))
of a spin 4-manifold
X
, where
k(X)
denotes the
k
-invariant corresponding to the second Postnikov stage of
X
. This is because, in the orientable case,
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{J}
is determined by
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}
; by the Serre exact sequence (
1.1
), being a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-null-homologous immersed sphere in
X
is precisely determined by being in the image of
\mathfrak{f}
, the map from the exact sequence (
1.1
), which is determined by
k(X)
(see
Appendix

A
); and
c
can be taken to be the unknot, since
X
is spin. This means that
C
could potentially be used to prove non-realisability of certain quadratic 2-types by spin 4-manifolds. We have not attempted to explore this avenue here.

Remark 1.3
.

The reader who in interested in an example of a calculation of
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)
(as in
A
) can look at
Proposition

6.8
, where we calculate the prototypical non-trivial example of this obstruction. This is for
\xi=\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{\pi},0)
and the
3
-manifold is either
Y=T^{3}
or
S^{1}\times K
for
K
a Klein bottle, depending on
w_{1}^{\pi}
.

Remark 1.4
.

In this paper we work entirely in the smooth category, since working in the topological category provides no extra generality. We elaborate on this now.

Firstly, every topological 3-manifold admits a smooth structure unique up to isotopy
[
3
,
22
,
2
]
, so we can freely assume that our input 3-manifolds are smooth. Secondly, assume that we have a smooth
\xi
-3-manifold
(Y,u_{Y})
which admits a
topological

\xi
-filling
(X,u_{X})
, where
\xi
is a potential normal 1-type for a 4-manifold. The relative Kirby-Siebenmann invariant
ks(X,Y)\in H_{4}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
can be modified to be zero by connected-summing with Freedman’s
E_{8}
manifold
[
8
]
, if necessary, and since
E_{8}
is a spin, simply-connected
4
-manifold the
\xi
structure
u_{X}
may be extended across the connected-sum (note that this is where we use that
\xi
is a normal 1-type). Then, by Freedman-Quinn’s stable smoothing theorem
[
7
, Theorem 8.6]
, there exists a smooth structure on our filling after taking sufficiently many connected-sums with
S^{2}\times S^{2}
. Again, since
S^{2}\times S^{2}
is a spin, simply-connected 4-manifold, there is no issue in extending the
\xi
-structure and hence we have produced a
smooth

\xi
-filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
.

1.1.
Background

Milnor
[
21
]
and Kirby
[
16
, VII, Theorem 3]
proved that the spin bordism group
\Omega_{3}^{\Spin}
is zero. In the proof, one actually produces a simply-connected filling. In Kirby’s proof, he starts by showing that there exists an oriented filling, and he then considers a representative for the second relative Stiefel-Whitney class. He then uses an explicit geometric argument to complete the spin null-bordism. One can interpret
A
to be a generalisation of this result for all normal 1-types, though if one specialises our proof to the simply-connected case one obtains a slightly different proof of
\Omega_{3}^{\Spin}=0
(the interested reader can follow through the proof of
A
, taking the normal 1-type to be
\xi(\pi=\{1\},0,0)
, and see the differences with Kirby’s proof).

In
[
12
,
13
]
, the authors work on a similar problem to this paper but for
\Omega_{4}^{\xi}
, in the special case that
\xi=\xi(\pi,0,0)
. Those works concern the uniqueness problem, i.e. whether two smooth 4-manifolds with normal 1-type
\xi
are stably diffeomorphic, rather than the existence problem studied in this paper. In that case, there are also primary, secondary and tertiary invariants, and the authors relate them to algebraic invariants in certain cases. A particularly interesting case is discussed in
[
13
]
, where the authors relate the 4-dimensional tertiary invariant to the Kervaire-Milnor invariant, a secondary intersection invariant (see
Section

3.4
). For us, our 3-dimensional tertiary invariant is a primary intersection invariant. We do not have a theoretical justification for why this shift occurs, but it would be very interesting to know if there is some general phenomenon at play.

In
[
10
]
, Kasprowski, Nicholson and the third-named author discuss geometric interpretations for each stage of the filtration of
\Omega_{4}^{\xi}
given by the JSS, much like how we have stated
A
. Though again, much like in
[
12
]
the emphasis is on the equivalence relation on the 4-manifolds involved, rather than on the 5-dimensional
\xi
-bordism itself. One might try to also consider the filtration stages of
\Omega_{3}^{\xi}
as giving equivalence relations on polarised 3-manifolds, but studying 3-manifolds up to these relations is potentially wrongheaded, since 3-manifolds are more naturally classified by geometric techniques developed separately to the high-dimensional theory.

1.2.
Conventions and notation

(1)

By JSS we mean the
James spectral sequence
, introduced in
[
27
]
, cf.
Appendix

B
.

(2)

We will be using a
B\Pin^{+}
-structure on the normal bundles of manifolds. Usually, manifolds are said to be
\Pin^{+}
(resp.
\Pin^{-}
) if their
tangential
Stiefel-Whitney classes satisfy
w^{t}_{2}=0
(resp.
w^{t}_{2}=(w_{1}^{t})^{2}
). We deal with normal bundles and so say that a manifold has a
\Pin^{+}
(resp.
\Pin^{-}
) structure if the Stiefel-Whitney classes of the normal bundle satisfy
w_{2}=0
(resp.
w_{2}=w_{1}^{2}
). Note that
w_{1}=w_{1}^{t}
and
w_{2}^{t}=w_{1}^{2}+w_{2}
and that the Wu formula for normal bundle Stiefel-Whitney classes is

x^{2}=w_{2}(M)\smile x\in H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2),

for
M
a possibly non-orientable 4-manifold.

(3)

Let
A
be a finitely presented abelian group. We write
B^{n}A
for the
n
-th iterated bar construction. This gives a model for the Eilenberg-MacLane space
K(A,n)
.

(4)

Let
X
be a compact space, let
\pi:\pi_{1}(X)
be a group and set
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
. We will use Hurewicz homomorphisms
h_{2},h_{2}^{\mathbb{Z}},h_{2}^{\Lambda}
with domains
\pi_{2}(X),\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}/2,\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
respectively.

1.3.
Organisation

In
Section

2
we recall the various notions of Kreck’s modified surgery theory that we require. In
Section

3
we define and discuss the modifications of the standard intersection invariants used in surgery theory, as well as recalling the Kervaire-Milnor invariant. In
Section

4
we use the modified intersection invariants to define our null-bordism obstructions. In
Section

5
we show that these obstructions are well-defined by working with the geometric differentials. In particular, we prove
B
and its corollary
C
. Finally, in
Section

6
we show that our obstructions are complete obstructions to the null-bordism question, i.e. we prove
A
. In
Appendix

A
we show a technical statement about a certain Serre exact sequence. In
Appendix

B
we extend the definition of the JSS to the non-orientable setting; this may be of independent interest.

1.4.
Acknowledgements

We thank Daniel Kasprowski for helpful discussions. The authors would like to thank the
Max Plank Institute for Mathematics in Bonn
for their hospitality and support throughout much of the work.

2.
Modified Surgery

2.1.
Normal
1
–types

Consider the space
BO
, which classifies stable vector bundles. Kreck’s modified surgery theory
[
18
]
provides a stable classification of manifolds by introducing certain approximation spaces
B
, which are equipped with a highly coconnected map to
BO
. The space
B
serves as an approximation to a given manifold
M
and is defined as a Moore–Postnikov approximation for the stable normal bundle
X\xrightarrow{\nu_{X}}BO
. We now give a brief overview of Kreck’s theory. Given a map
\xi\colon B\to BO
a
\xi
-structure on a smooth manifold
X
is a map
\overline{\nu}_{X}\colon X\to B
, which lifts the normal bundle
\nu_{X}
over
\xi
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BO}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\nu}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{X}}

We call a pair
(X,\overline{\nu}_{X})
a
\xi
-manifold if
\overline{\nu}_{X}
is a
\xi
-structure on
X
although we sometimes drop
\overline{\nu}_{X}
from the notation. A
\xi
-diffeomorphism of two
\xi
-manifolds is a diffeomorphism of the underlying manifolds that commutes with the
\xi
-structures up to homotopy over
BO
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\xi\colon B\to\BO
be a map from a CW-complex
B
of finite type. We say that
\xi
is a
universal fibration
if it is a 2-coconnected fibration. In particular, this means that
\xi_{*}\colon\pi_{k}(B)\to\pi_{k}(\BO)
is injective for
k=2
and an isomorphism for
k\geq 3
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\xi\colon B\to\BO
be a universal fibration and let
\nu_{X}\colon X\to\BO
be the stable normal bundle for a compact, smooth
4
-manifold
X
. We say that
\xi
is a
normal 1-type
for
X
if there exists a
\xi
-structure
(X,\overline{\nu}_{X})
such that
\overline{\nu}_{X}\colon X\to B
is 2-connected. We call such a choice of
\overline{\nu}_{X}
a
normal 1-smoothing
.

The following definition together with
Theorem

2.4
classifies all possible normal
1
-types of
4
-manifolds.

Definition 2.3
.

[
27
]

Let
\pi
be a finitely presented group, let
w_{1}^{\pi}\in H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
and
w_{2}^{\pi}\in H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cup\{\infty\}
be given. Then define
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
to be given by the homotopy pullback of:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BO}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})}

\lrcorner

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}^{\pi}\times w_{2}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}\times w_{2}}

if
w_{2}^{\pi}\neq\infty
and

\hbox to129.42pt{\vbox to49.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 76.71555pt\lower-24.73613pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-9.26486pt}{-21.44305pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

if
w_{2}^{\pi}=\infty
. In both cases we first realise the map
w_{1}^{\pi}\times w_{2}^{\pi}
resp.
w_{1}^{\pi}
as fibrations, so the maps
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
resp.
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},\infty)
are fibrations.

In this paper we sometimes refer to
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
as a normal
1
-type without referencing any particular manifold. By this we mean a normal structure
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})\colon B\to BO
defined by elements in the cohomology of
B\pi
as above. We do this when we seek to consider
4
-manifolds for which
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
is the normal
1
-type.

For a given manifold we use the following theorem to decide its normal structure.

Theorem 2.4

(Various normal 1-types, Teichner
[
27
]
)
.

Let
X
be a compact, smooth
4
-manifold with
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
and let
\widetilde{X}
denote the universal cover of
X
.

(1)

If
w_{2}(\widetilde{X})\neq 0
, then the normal 1-type of
X
is
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},\infty)
, where
w_{1}^{\pi}\in H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the preimage of
w_{1}(X)
,

(2)

If
w_{2}(\widetilde{X})=0
, then the normal
1
-type of
X
is
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
, where
w_{1}^{\pi}
and
w_{2}^{\pi}
are the preimages of
w_{1}(X)
and
w_{2}(X)
, respectively.

Remark 2.5
.

Let
\pi
be a finitely presented group and let
w_{1}^{\pi}\in H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Then a
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},0)
-structure on a manifold
X
is a
\Pin^{+}
-structure on
X
along with any
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism
u_{X}^{\pi}\colon X\to B\pi
such that
u_{X}^{\pi*}(w_{1}^{\pi})=w_{1}(X)
. In the case that
w_{1}^{\pi}=0
this is equivalent to a
\Spin
-structure on
X
and any
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism
u_{X}^{\pi}\colon X\to B\pi
.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
i\colon\xi\to\xi^{\prime}
be a map of normal structures, i.e. a map of total spaces over
BO
, and let
(Y^{n},u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-manifold. Then we say that a
\xi^{\prime}
-manifold
(X^{n+1},u_{X})
with an identification
\partial N\xrightarrow[\cong]{\varphi}M
is a
\xi^{\prime}
-filling of

M
if
u_{N}|_{\partial N}
is homotopic to
i\circ u_{M}\circ\varphi^{-1}
rel
BO
.

We will particularly use the above definition when
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
and
\xi^{\prime}=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},\infty)
, with the map between them given by the projection
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
and using the universal property for homotopy pullbacks. In this case, we refer to the
\xi^{\prime}
-filling
(N,u_{N}^{\pi})
as a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-
filling
(in the case of a closed manifold we will simply refer to it as a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-
manifold
).

We have the following ‘surgery below the middle dimension’ lemma in our relative setting.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type,
(Y^{3},u_{Y})
a
\xi
-3-manifold and
X
its
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling. Then there exists
(X^{\prime},u_{X}^{\pi}\colon X^{\prime}\to B\pi)
a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
such that
x_{X}^{\pi}
is a
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism.

Proof.

First make the map
u_{X}^{\pi}
surjective on
\pi_{1}
as follows. Let
G:=\{g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{k}\}
be a generating set for
\pi
. Form the connected-sum of

\#_{G}S^{1}\times S^{3}\xrightarrow{\sqcup u_{g_{i}}}B\pi

with
X
, where the
u_{g_{i}}
are homotopy classes of maps which send the generators of the
\pi_{1}(S^{1}\times S^{3})
-factors onto the
g_{i}
.

Now surger out the kernel of
\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi
following the usual procedure, see e.g
[
31
,
18
]
.
∎

2.2.
Obstruction theory for
\xi
-structures

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
with
w_{2}^{\pi}\neq\infty
be a normal
1
-type. We wish to define an obstruction for
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifolds to admit a
\xi
-structure extending the one given on a boundary.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a, possibly empty,
\xi
-3-manifold and let
(X,u_{X}^{\pi}\colon X\to B\pi)
be its
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling. Then there exists a unique obstruction
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{y},u_{X}^{\pi})\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)
that vanishes if and only if
u_{Y}
extends to a
\xi
-structure on
X
.

Furthermore, if
Y=\emptyset
then
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,\emptyset,\emptyset,u_{X}^{\pi})
splits as a sum
w_{2}(M)+u_{M}^{\pi*}(w_{2}^{\pi})
.

Proof.

Consider the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\BO}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{Y}}

\lrcorner

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}^{\pi}\times w_{2}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{X}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}\times w_{2}}

The outermost square already commutes up to homotopy since
u_{Y}
is a
\xi
-structure. The triangle starting at
X
does not necessarily commute, but it does after postcomposition with the projection
B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
since
X
is a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling. Hence we only have to consider the diagram after projecting to the
B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
-factor.

The middle square comes with a homotopy
B\times I\to B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
making the square commute. Define the homotopy
\mathscr{H}\colon Y\times I\to B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
using precomposition. Using the
H
-space structure on
B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
, the difference of the maps
w_{2}(X)\colon X\to B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
and
(u_{X}^{\pi})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi})\colon X\to B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
defines a map

w_{2}(X)-(u_{X}^{\pi})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi})\colon X\to B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2.

The homotopy
\mathscr{H}
then gives a trivialisation of
w_{2}(X)-(u_{X}^{\pi})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi})
precomposed with the inclusion
Y\to X
, and hence defines a relative cohomology class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{y},u_{X}^{\pi})\in H_{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. It is clear from the construction that this cohomology class has the desired properties.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

In particular, for
w_{1}^{\pi}=0=w_{2}^{\pi}
, a
u_{Y}
-structure on
Y
includes a
\Spin
structure on
Y
and the obstruction
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{y},u_{X}^{\pi})
coincides with the relative Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}^{R}
defined by Kervaire
[
15
]
.

Definition 2.10
.

If
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
is a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold then we set
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,u_{X}^{\pi}):=\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,\emptyset,\emptyset,u_{X}^{\pi})
. If
(X_{1},u_{X_{1}}^{\pi}),(X_{2},u_{X_{2}}^{\pi})
are two
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-fillings of
Y
when we use the relative Mayer-Vietoris sequence to show that the map

H^{2}(X_{1}\cup X_{2},Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2}(X_{1},Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus H^{2}(X_{2},Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)

is an isomorphism. We define
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X_{1}\cup X_{2},Y,u_{Y},u_{X_{1}\cup X_{2}}^{\pi})
to be the preimage of the sum
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X_{1},Y,u_{Y},u_{X_{1}}^{\pi})+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X_{2},Y,u_{Y},u_{X_{2}}^{\pi})
.

In
Lemma

2.11
we identify the obstruction
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}
of manifolds obtained from gluing two manifolds along a common boundary.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
,
w_{2}^{\pi}\neq\infty
be a normal
1
-type. Let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-3- manifold. Let
X_{1},X_{2}
be two
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-fillings of
Y
. Then the inclusion maps
X_{1},X_{2}\hookrightarrow X_{1}\cup\overline{X_{2}}
induce an equality

PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X_{1},Y,u_{Y},u_{X_{1}}^{\pi}))+PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(\overline{X_{2}},Y,u_{Y},u_{X_{2}}^{\pi}))=PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X_{1}\cup\overline{X_{2}},u_{X_{1}\cup X_{2}}^{\pi})).

Proof.

Write
M:=X_{1}\cup\overline{X_{2}}
. The following diagram, where the horizontal maps come from the relevant Mayer-Vietoris sequences and the unlabelled vertical map comes from the long exact sequence of the pair, commutes, (use naturality of cap product).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(M,Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(X_{1},Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus H^{2}(X_{2},Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(X_{1};\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus H_{2}(X_{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+}

Now the class
(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X_{1},Y,u_{Y},u_{X_{1}}^{\pi}),\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X_{2},Y,u_{Y},u_{X_{2}}^{\pi}))
maps, under the top horizontal isomorphism to the obstruction class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,Y,u_{Y},u_{M}^{\pi})
for
M
to have a
\xi
-structure, which restricts to
u_{Y}
on
Y
. This class in turn maps to
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
, the obstruction to
M
having a
\xi
-structure itself. Now the commutativity of the diagram concludes the proof since the bottom map, is just the summing map, since our coefficients are
\mathbb{Z}/2
.
∎

Definition 2.12
.

A class
c\in\pi_{2}(X)
is
s-characteristic (spherical characteristic)
, if for the
\pmod{2}
augmentation map
\varepsilon_{2}\colon\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/2
we have

\varepsilon_{2}\lambda(c,\alpha)=\varepsilon_{2}\lambda(\alpha,\alpha),

for all classes
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)
. Similarly,
w\in\pi_{2}(X)
is called
r-characteristic
(
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
-characteristic
) if
Q(h_{2}(w),\alpha)=Q(\alpha,\alpha)
for all classes
\alpha\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
representable by maps
\alpha\colon\mathbb{RP}^{2}\to X
, where
Q
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-valued intersection form on
X
.

Note that for two spherical classes
\alpha,\beta\in\pi_{2}(X)
we have
Q(h_{2}(\alpha),h_{2}(\beta))=\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(\alpha,\beta))
, showing the analogy between the definitions of
s-
and
r-
characteristic elements above.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type. Let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-3-manifold and let
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
be a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling with
u_{X}^{\pi}
a
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism (see
Lemma

2.7
). Let
w\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
be the Poincaré dual of the class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
defined above. Assume that
w
is a spherical class. Then any lift
c\in\pi_{2}(X)
of
w
is
s-characteristic
.

Proof.

We will first show the lemma in the case that
Y=\emptyset
. Let
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)
. Let
Q
be the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-intersection form on
H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Then
\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(c,\alpha))=Q(w,h_{2}(\alpha))=\langle\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,u_{X}^{\pi}),h_{2}(\alpha)\rangle
. By
Proposition

2.8
,
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
splits as a sum
(u_{X}^{\pi})^{*}w_{2}^{\pi}+w_{2}(X)
. The term
\left<u_{X}^{\pi*}(w_{2}^{\pi}),h_{2}(\alpha)\right>=0
vanishes because
u_{M*}^{\pi}h_{2}=0
. Now

\left<u_{X}^{\pi*}(w_{2}^{\pi})+w_{2}(X),h_{2}(\alpha)\right>=\left<w_{2}(X),h_{2}(\alpha)\right>=Q(h_{2}(\alpha),h_{2}(\alpha))=\varepsilon_{2}\lambda(\alpha,\alpha)

since
w_{2}(X)
is characteristic, which proves the claim.

For the general case
X
, with boundary
Y
, let
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)
be any element and consider the double
DX
along with the map
u^{\pi}_{DX}:=u^{\pi}_{X}\cup u^{\pi}_{X}\colon DX\to B\pi
. Fix one of the inclusions
X\to DX
and denote the switch map
DX\to DX
by
D
. The dual of the obstruction class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(DX,u_{DX}^{\pi})
is the sum
w_{D}=w+Dw
according to
Lemma

2.11
. Since
w
lifts to
c
, we can realise a lift of
w_{D}
as the ambient connected sum
c\#Dc
. We get

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda^{X}(\alpha,c))=\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda^{DX}(\alpha,c))=\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda^{DX}(\alpha,c+Dc))=\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda^{DX}(\alpha,\alpha))=\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda^{X}(\alpha,\alpha)).

Where the first and last equality follows by naturality of the map
X\to DX
, second equality holds since
\alpha
and
Dc
are disjoint and third by the closed case.
∎

3.
Intersections and self intersections

Throughout this section let
X
be a compact 4-manifold, let
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
, let
\Lambda:=\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
, and let
w_{1}=w_{1}(X)\colon\pi\to\mathbb{Z}/2
be the first Stiefel-Whitney class.

3.1.
Preliminaries

Recall there is a map

\mu\colon\pi_{2}(X)\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]/(g-w_{1}(g)g^{-1},1)

given by choosing an immersed representative for
\alpha
and then calculating the self-intersections (counting group elements), ignoring those with the trivial group element (see
[
31
, Theorem 5.2]
) to make this not depend on the choice of representative
1
1

1

If one is given an immersed representative
\widetilde{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)
, we will write
\mu(\widetilde{\alpha})\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi]/(g-w_{1}(g)g^{-1})
, where we do not have to mod out by this indeterminacy.
. The indeterminacy
g\sim w_{1}(g)g^{-1}
comes from there not being a preferred ordering of the sheets at a self-intersection point. We define an altered version of this invariant.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\widetilde{\alpha}\colon S^{2}\to X
be an immersed sphere with
n
self intersections. Let
\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha})
be a set of
n
ordered pairs of points
a_{i},b_{i}\in S^{2}
for
i=1,\dots,n
such that
\widetilde{\alpha}(a_{i})=\widetilde{\alpha}(b_{i})
. Assume
\mathfrak{o}
spans all the double points. Then we call a pair
(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha}))
an
immersed sphere with ordered double points
.

Now for any immersed sphere with ordered double points
(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha}))
the self intersection
\mu(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha}))
is well-defined in
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
.

Definition 3.2
.

Let

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}\colon\pi_{2}(X)\to I/(g-1-w_{1}(g)(g^{-1}-1)).

be defined as follows. Let
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)
. Represent
\alpha
as an immersed sphere with ordered double points
(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha}))
.
Now define
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)=[\mu(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha}))-\varepsilon(\mu(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha})))]
, where the brackets represent taking the quotient.

We note that in the case that
X
is orientable the above definition is equivalent to
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\widetilde{\alpha})-\varepsilon(\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\widetilde{\alpha}))
since
\varepsilon
factors through
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]/(g-w_{1}(g)g^{-1})
if and only if
X
is
w_{1}=0
.

Proposition 3.3
.

The function of
Definition

3.2

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}\colon\pi_{2}(X)\to I/(g-1-w_{1}(g)(g^{-1}-1)).

is well-defined.

Proof.

First we show that it is independent on the choice of
\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha})
. Let
\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}(\widetilde{\alpha})
be
\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha})
with one ordered pair
(a_{i},b_{i})
swapped and assume that the group element at
(a_{i},b_{i})
dictated by
\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha})
is
g\in\pi
. then the element at
(a_{i},b_{i})
is dictated by
w_{1}(g)g^{-1}
and we get

\displaystyle\mu(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha}))-\varepsilon(\mu(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha})))-\mu(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}(\widetilde{\alpha}))+\varepsilon(\mu(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}^{\prime}(\widetilde{\alpha})))=

\displaystyle=g-w_{1}(g)g^{-1}-\varepsilon(g)+\varepsilon(w_{1}(g)g^{-1})=

\displaystyle=g-w_{1}(g)g^{-1}-1+w_{1}(g)=g-1-w_{1}(g)(g^{-1}-1),

which vanishes in
I/(g-1-w_{1}(g)(g^{-1}-1))
.
The proof that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}
does not depend on the choice of immersion is the same as in
[
31
]
.
∎

Analogously to
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}
, we consider the following
\mathbb{Z}
-intersection form

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}:\pi_{2}(X)\times\pi_{2}(X)\to I

defined as
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\alpha,\beta)=\lambda(\alpha,\beta)-\varepsilon(\lambda(\alpha,\beta))
where
\lambda:\pi_{2}(X)\times\pi_{2}(X)\to\Lambda
is the usual equivariant intersection form. This form was considered in
[
17
]
.

The main downside of this form is that we lose the
\Lambda
-hermitian structure, in particular the
\Lambda
-bilinearity of the usual form
\lambda
.

Lemma 3.4
.

The form
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}
is
\mathbb{Z}
-quadratic and the form
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinear form associated to the (underlying)
\mathbb{Z}
-quadratic form
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}
, i.e. for all
\alpha,\beta\in\pi_{2}(X)

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha+\beta)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\beta)+[{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\alpha,\beta)]\in I/({g-1-w_{1}(g)(g^{-1}-1))}.

Proof.

We leave to the reader to show that that
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinear form and that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(n\alpha)=n^{2}\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\alpha,\beta\in\pi_{2}(X)
, let
(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha}))
and
(\widetilde{\beta},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\beta}))
be immersed sphere representatives of
\alpha
and
\beta
with ordered double points. We can naturally form an immersed sphere with ordered double points
(\widetilde{\alpha}+\widetilde{\beta},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha})\cup\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\beta})\cup\mathfrak{o}_{\widetilde{\alpha}+\widetilde{\beta}})
, where
\mathfrak{o}_{\widetilde{\alpha}+\widetilde{\beta}}
is the ordering of the new intersection points between
\widetilde{\alpha}
and
\widetilde{\beta}
given by ordering the pair
(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\beta})
. We calculate

\displaystyle\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha+\beta)

\displaystyle=[\mu(\widetilde{\alpha}+\widetilde{\beta},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha})\cup\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\beta})\cup\mathfrak{o}_{\widetilde{\alpha}+\widetilde{\beta}})-\varepsilon(\mu(\widetilde{\alpha}+\widetilde{\beta},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha})\cup\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\beta})\cup\mathfrak{o}_{\widetilde{\alpha}+\widetilde{\beta}}))]

\displaystyle=[\mu(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha}))+\mu(\widetilde{\beta},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\beta}))+\lambda(\alpha,\beta)-\varepsilon(\mu(\widetilde{\alpha},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\alpha})))-\varepsilon(\mu(\widetilde{\beta},\mathfrak{o}(\widetilde{\beta})))-\varepsilon(\lambda(\alpha,\beta))]

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\beta)+[\lambda(\alpha,\beta)-\varepsilon(\lambda(\alpha,\beta))]

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\beta)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\alpha,\beta),

where the square brackets represent the corresponding quotient.
∎

We will use the involution on the group ring
\overline{\cdot}\colon\mathbb{Z}[\pi]\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
defined by linearly extending
\overline{g}=w_{1}(g)g^{-1}
for
g\in\pi
to the whole group ring. Recall that, given
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)
, the sum
\mu(\alpha)+\overline{\mu(\alpha)}
lies in
\mathbb{Z}[\pi]/(1)
, since the indeterminacy due to the sheet choices cancels out. As an easy consequence, the sum
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)+\overline{\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)}
lies in
I
. Using this fact, the forms
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}
and
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}
satisfy the following relation.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)
we have

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\alpha,\alpha)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)+\overline{\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)}\in I.

Proof.

Pick an immersed representative
\widetilde{\alpha}
for
\alpha
. By Wall
[
31
, Theorem 5.2]
, we have the corresponding version for the original
\mu
and
\lambda
, i.e.

(3.1)

\lambda(\alpha,\alpha)=\mu(\widetilde{\alpha})+\overline{\mu(\widetilde{\alpha})}+e(\widetilde{\alpha}),

where
e(\widetilde{\alpha})
is the Euler number of the immersion
\widetilde{\alpha}
. This gives

\displaystyle{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\alpha,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lambda(\alpha,\alpha)-\varepsilon(\lambda(\alpha,\alpha))

\displaystyle=\mu(\widetilde{\alpha})+\overline{\mu(\widetilde{\alpha})}+e(\widetilde{\alpha})-\varepsilon(\lambda(\alpha,\alpha))

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)+\varepsilon(\mu(\widetilde{\alpha}))+\overline{\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)}+\varepsilon(\overline{\mu(\widetilde{\alpha})})+e(\widetilde{\alpha})-\varepsilon(\lambda(\alpha,\alpha))

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)+\overline{\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\alpha)},

where the first equality is by the definition of
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}
, the second by
Equation

3.1
, the third by the definition of
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}
, and the fourth by applying
\varepsilon
to
Equation

3.1
.
∎

3.2.
The form
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}

We are interested in making the self-intersection form
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}
valued in
H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. To do this, we need a map
I\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
which vanishes on the ideal
(g-1-w_{1}(g)(g^{-1}-1))\subset I
. In that direction, we prove the following.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\pi
be a group,
I
resp.
J
be the
\mathbb{Z}
resp.
\mathbb{Z}/2
augmentation ideals. Then we have the following:

(1)

IJ=JI
.

(2)

For any right
\Lambda
-module
M
there are isomorphisms

•

M\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\cong M/MJ
given by
m\otimes 1\mapsto m
,

•

and
M\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}\cong M/MI
given by
m\otimes 1\mapsto m
.

(3)

For all
i\in I
we have
i^{2},2i\in IJ
.

(4)

There is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}/2
-vector spaces
H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong I/IJ
induced by the map sending
g\in\pi
to
g-1\in I
. The inverse of this map is given as follows. The ideal
I
, as an abelian group is generated by elements
\{g-1\mid g\in\pi\}
. We define the map by sending
\sum_{i}(g_{i}-1)+\sum_{j}(1-g_{j})\in I
to
\prod_{i}g_{i}\prod_{j}g_{j}^{-1}
.

Proof.

For (
1
), let
i\in I
and
j\in J
. Then

ij=i(j-\varepsilon(j))+i\varepsilon(j)=i(j-\varepsilon(j))+\varepsilon(j)i

Since
j-\varepsilon(j)\in I\subset J
we find that
ij\in JI
. We apply an analogous calculation to show that
ji\in IJ
.

For the first isomorphism of (
2
), the
\Lambda
-bilinear map
M\times\mathbb{Z}/2\to M/MJ
given by
(m,k)\mapsto mk
is well-defined since
2\in J
and so
m2\in MJ
. This map descends to a map
M\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\to M/MJ
. It is clearly surjective. If
m\otimes 1
is mapped to zero then
m\in MJ
and hence
m=m^{\prime}j
. Now
m^{\prime}j\otimes 1=m^{\prime}\otimes\varepsilon_{2}(j)=0
.

The proof of the second isomorphism is analogous.

For (
3
) we have
I\subset J
and so
i^{2}\in IJ
. Additionally we have
2\in J
which proves the claim.

For (
4
) consider the short exact sequence:

0\to I\to\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}.

Tensoring the sequence with
\mathbb{Z}/2
gives the long exact sequence

\Tor_{1}^{\Lambda}(\Lambda,\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\Tor_{1}^{\Lambda}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}/2)\to I\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\to\Lambda\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\to 0

where the second term is isomorphic to
H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. On the right side of the sequence we have the isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/2\cong\Lambda\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, so we obtain an exact sequence

\Tor_{1}^{\Lambda}(\Lambda,\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to I\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\to 0.

Since the term
\Tor_{1}^{\Lambda}(\Lambda,\mathbb{Z}/2)
vanishes, the map
H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to I\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
is an isomorphism. Postcomposing with the isomorphism from (
2
), we obtain an isomorphism
\psi\colon H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to I/IJ
, which we now describe. Let
B
be a CW-complex with a single
0
cell and, and one
1
-cell for each generator in some fixed generating set
G
of
\pi
. Let
E\pi
be the universal cover, which is a contractive space and we can take its chain complex
C_{3}(E\pi)\to C_{2}(E\pi)\to C_{1}(E\pi)\to C_{0}(E\pi)
as the free resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
. Note that
C_{0}(E\pi)\cong\Lambda
and
C_{1}(E\pi)\cong\prod_{g\in G}\Lambda
.

We have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}(E\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}(E\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(E\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}(E\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{g\in G}\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}^{g}\to g-1}

Upon tensoring the rows with
\mathbb{Z}/2
and taking homology we find that the map

H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\Tor^{\Lambda}_{1}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\ker(I\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\to\Lambda\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong I\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2

is given by
g\to(g-1)\otimes 1
, so
\psi\colon H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to I/IJ
is given by
g\mapsto g-1
. The map
\psi
is a map of
\mathbb{Z}/2
-vector spaces because

\displaystyle g_{1}+g_{2}=g_{1}g_{2}\mapsto g_{1}g_{2}-1

\displaystyle=(g_{1}-1)(g_{2}+1)+g_{1}-g_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv g_{1}+g_{2}\equiv(g_{1}-1)+(g_{2}-1)\pmod{IJ}.\qed

This produces a map
I\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
given by quotienting by
IJ
. We prove the following fact about the kernel of this quotient map, which will be useful in
Section

6
. The reader can skip this for now and move onto the definition of
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\pi
be a finitely presented group. Then the kernel of the map

I/({g-1-w_{1}(g)(g^{-1}-1)})\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)

defined in
Lemma

3.6
(
4
) is generated as a
\Lambda
-module by

\{g_{i}g_{j}-g_{i}-g_{j}+1,2g_{i}-2\mid g_{i},g_{j}\in\pi\},

where the module structure is given by conjugation.

Proof.

From
Lemma

3.6
(
4
) we know that the kernel of the map
I\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is
IJ
. Now we have a factorisation

I\xrightarrow{q}\mathrel{\mkern-14.0mu}\rightarrow I/({g-1-w_{1}(g)(g^{-1}-1)})\xrightarrow{f}H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)

Using the surjectivity of
q
we show that
q(IJ)=\ker(f)
. The module
I
is generated (as an abelian group) by the set
\{g-1\mid g\in\pi\}
and the module
J
is generated (as an abelian group) by
\{g-1\mid g\in\pi\}\cup\{2\}
. It follows that the modules
IJ
and
p(IJ)
are generated by the product of pairs of the above generators
\{g_{i}g_{j}-g_{i}-g_{j}+2\mid g_{i},g_{j}\in\pi\}\cup\{2g-2\mid g\in\pi\}
.
∎

We now define
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
using
Lemma

3.6
. Note that since
g-g^{-1}=(g+1)(g^{-1}-1)\in IJ
and
g+g^{-1}-2=(g+1)(1-g^{-1})\in I^{2}\subset IJ
we have that
(g-1-w_{1}(g)(g^{-1}-1))\subset IJ
. Hence we can define
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}\colon\pi_{2}(X)\to I/IJ
, simply by postcomposing
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}
by the quotient map.

Definition 3.8
.

Let

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}\colon\pi_{2}(X)\to I/IJ

be defined as
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}
postcomposed with the quotient map
I/((g-1-w_{1}(g)(g^{-1}-1))\to I/IJ
. Similarly define

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}:\pi_{2}(X)\times\pi_{2}(X)\to I/IJ

as
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}
postcomposed with the quotient map
I\to I/IJ
.

We have the following refinement of these forms.

Proposition 3.9
.

The forms
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
and
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}
of
Definition

3.8
induce a well-defined
\mathbb{Z}/2
-quadratic form and (an underlying)
\mathbb{Z}/2
-bilinear form on
\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\left(\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}/2\right)\times\left(\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}/2\right)
, respectively.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}/2
. Using that
\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}\cong\pi_{2}(X)
and the long exact sequence obtained by tensoring the sequence
\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2
using
\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}-
shows that different lifts
\overline{\alpha}\in\pi_{2}(X)
of
\alpha
differ by
2\pi_{2}(X)
. Let
2\beta\in 2\pi_{2}(X)
. By the following calculation

[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\overline{\alpha}+2\beta)]=[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\overline{\alpha})]+[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(2\beta)]+[{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\overline{\alpha},2\beta)]=[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(\overline{\alpha})]

We see that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
does not depend on the choice of lift. Here
[-]
denotes the projection
I/g\sim g^{-1}\to I/IJ
, and the last equality holds since
I/IJ
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-vector space by
Lemma

3.6
(
4
).

Similarly for
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}/2
, their lifts
\overline{\alpha},\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}\in\pi_{2}(X)
their lifts, pick
2\beta,2\beta^{\prime}\in 2\pi_{2}(X)
and so we have

[{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\overline{\alpha}+2\beta,\overline{\alpha^{\prime}}+2\beta^{\prime})]={\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\overline{\alpha},\overline{\alpha^{\prime}})

again using
I/IJ
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2
- vector space. The rest of the claim follows from
Lemma

3.4
.
∎

The induced forms from
Proposition

3.9
will still be denoted by
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
and
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}
. We have the following algebraic fact which will be useful when establishing the properties of
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
and
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
u\in J^{2}
and
u\in I
. Then
u\in IJ
.

Proof.

We can write
u=j_{1}j_{2}
for
j_{1},j_{2}\in J
. Since
u\in I
we have that
\varepsilon(j_{1}j_{2})=0
and so either
\varepsilon(j_{1})=0
or
\varepsilon(j_{2})=0
and so either
j_{1}\in I
or
j_{2}\in I
. Now use that
IJ=JI
(
Lemma

3.6
(
1
)).
∎

We now state and prove the various properties that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
and
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}
satisfy.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
g\in\pi
,
a,b\in\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}/2
,
j\in J
and
i\in I
. Then the following equations hold in
I/IJ
.

(1)

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(ga)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)
.

(2)

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(a,a)=0
.

(3)

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(ga,a)=(g-1)\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,a))
.

(4)

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(ja,b)=(j-\varepsilon(j))\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,b))
.

(5)

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(ja+b)=(j-\varepsilon(j))\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,a)+\lambda(a,b))+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(b)
.

Proof.

For (
1
), note that
g^{-1}-g=(g^{-1}-1)(g+1)\in IJ
and so
g\equiv g^{-1}\pmod{IJ}
. We have that

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(ga)=g\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)g^{-1}=(g-1)\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)g+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)g\equiv\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)(g-1)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)\equiv\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)\pmod{IJ},

by using
Proposition

3.9
and that
(g-1)\in J,\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)\in I
. This completes the proof of (
1
).

For (
2
) we calculate
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(2a)=2\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(a,a)
. Since
I/IJ
is
2
-torsion immediately obtain that
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(a,a)=0
. This completes the proof of (
2
).

For (
3
) we calculate that

\displaystyle{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(ga,a)

\displaystyle=g\lambda(a,a)-\varepsilon g\lambda(a,a)

\displaystyle=g({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(a,a)+\varepsilon(\lambda(a,a)))-\varepsilon\lambda(a,a)

\displaystyle=g{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(a,a)+(g-1)(\varepsilon(\lambda(a,a))-\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,a)))+(g-1)\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,a))

\displaystyle=(g-1)\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,a)),

where the first and second equalities follow from the definition of
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}
, the third by using
(g-1)(\varepsilon(\lambda(a,a))-\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,a)))\in IJ
, and the last by (
2
). This completes the proof of (
3
).

For (
4
) we let
j\in J
. We can express
j
as
i-y
for
y=\varepsilon(j)
an even integer and
i\in I
. Then
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(ja,b)={\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(ia,b)+2{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(a,b)={\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(ia,b)
in
IJ
, so it is sufficient to assume that
j=i\in I
. Write
i
as a sum of
n
expressions of the form
(g-1)
and
(1-g)
for
g\in\pi
. We will proceed by the induction on
n
. If
n=0
then
i=0
and there is nothing to prove.

If
n=1
then
i=1-g
or
i=g-1
. The proof will be the same in all cases so we assume the former. We obtain

\displaystyle{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}((g-1)a,b)

\displaystyle={\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(ga,b)-{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(a,b)

\displaystyle=\lambda(ga,b)-\varepsilon\lambda(ga,b)-(\lambda(a,b)-\varepsilon(\lambda(a,b))

\displaystyle=g\lambda(a,b)-\varepsilon\lambda(a,b)-\lambda(a,b)+\varepsilon\lambda(a,b)

\displaystyle=(g-1)\lambda(a,b)

\displaystyle=(g-1)(\lambda(a,b)-\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,b)))+(g-1)\varepsilon_{2}\lambda(a,b)

\displaystyle\equiv(g-1)\varepsilon_{2}\lambda(a,b)\pmod{IJ},

where in the sixth line we used that
\lambda(a,b)-\varepsilon(\lambda(a,b))\in J
.

For the induction step we take
i=(g-1)+i^{\prime}
or
i=(1-g)+i^{\prime\prime}
where the number of summands of
i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime}\in I
is smaller than the number of summands in
i
. In the former case the induction step holds since

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(((g-1)+i^{\prime})a,b)={\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}((g-1)a,b)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(i^{\prime}a,b)\equiv(g-1+i^{\prime})\varepsilon_{2}\lambda(a,b),

by using
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinearity of
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}
. The other case is similar. This completes the proof of (
4
).

For (
5
) we can proceed as above, reducing to the case that
j\in I
since

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(-\varepsilon(j)a+ja+b)=\varepsilon(j)^{2}\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(a)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(ja+b)+\varepsilon(j){\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(a,ja+b)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(ja+b),

using that
\varepsilon(j)
is even.

We will do the same induction on the number of summands in
j=i
as in (
4
). Again, the claim is trivial for
i=0
.

Assume
i=g-1
(if
i=1-g
the proof is analogous). We calculate

\displaystyle\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}((g-1)a+b)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}((g-1)a)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(b)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}((g-1)a,b)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(ga)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(-a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(ga,-a)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(b)+(g-1)\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,b))

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(ga,a)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(b)+(g-1)\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,b))

\displaystyle=(g-1)\varepsilon_{2}((\lambda(a,a)+\lambda(a,b))+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(b),

where the first and second equalities follow from
Proposition

3.9
, the third from (
1
) and that
I/IJ
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-vector space, and the fourth by (
3
) and that
I/IJ
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-vector space. This proves the base case.

For the induction step assume
i=(g-1)+i^{\prime}
for a non-zero element
i^{\prime}\in I
which has fewer summands then
i
. We obtain that

\displaystyle\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(ia+b)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(i^{\prime}a+b)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}((g-1)a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(j^{\prime}a+b,(g-1)a)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(i^{\prime}a+b)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(ga)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(ga,a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(j^{\prime}a,(g-1)a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(b,(g-1)a)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(i^{\prime}a+b)+2\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(ga,a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(i^{\prime}a,(g-1)a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(b,(g-1)a)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(i^{\prime}a+b)+(g-1)\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,a))+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(i^{\prime}a,(g-1)a)+(g-1)\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,b)),

where the first and second equality follow from
Proposition

3.9
, the third follows from (
1
) and (
2
), and the last follows from (
4
). Showing
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(i^{\prime}a,(g-1)a)=0
will complete the proof of the claim, which we do in the following sequence of equations.

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(i^{\prime}a,(g-1)a)=i^{\prime}\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(a,(g-1)a))=i^{\prime}\varepsilon_{2}(g\lambda(a,a))-\lambda(a,a))=0.

This completes the proof of (
5
).
∎

3.3.
The forms
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
and
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}
in relation to normal 1-types

Ideally we would like
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
and
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}
to be forms on the group
\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
. This will not be possible, but if we make some assumptions on our inputs we can push these forms to be well-defined on
\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Fix a normal
1
-type
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
, let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a (potentially empty)
\xi
-3-manifold and let
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
be a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling. Then the following hold.

(1)

Let
c\in\pi_{2}(X)
be s-characteristic. Then
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)
only depends on the image
[c]\in\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
.

(2)

Let
\omega\in\pi_{2}\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
such that
h^{\Lambda}_{2}(\omega)=0
. Then
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(-,\omega)\colon\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\to I/IJ
is well-defined.

Proof.

For (
1
) note that different lifts
c\in\pi_{2}(X)
of elements in
\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
differ by elements in
J\pi_{2}(X)
. Let
\beta\in\pi_{2}(X)
and
j\in J
. Then

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(c+j\beta)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(c)+j-\varepsilon(j)\varepsilon_{2}((\lambda(\beta,\beta))+\lambda(\beta,c))=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(c)

where the first equality is by (
5
) of
Lemma

3.11
and the second holds since
c
is s-characteristic, implying that
\varepsilon_{2}((\lambda(\beta,\beta))+\lambda(\beta,c))=0
.

For (
2
) choose a lift
\overline{\omega}\in\pi_{2}(X)
of
\omega
and an element
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}
along with its lift
\overline{\alpha}\in\pi_{2}(X)
. We need to show that the value
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\overline{\alpha},\overline{\omega})
does not depend on the choices above. Different choices of
\overline{\omega},\overline{\alpha}
differ by
j\gamma
and
j^{\prime}\beta
for
\beta\in\ker(h_{2}^{\Lambda})
,
\gamma\in\pi_{2}(X)
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
. We obtain that

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\alpha+j\beta,\omega+j^{\prime}\gamma)={\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\alpha,\omega)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(i\beta,\omega)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\alpha,i^{\prime}\gamma)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(i\beta,i^{\prime}\gamma),

and using (
4
) of
Lemma

3.11
we get
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(i\beta,\omega)=i\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(\beta,\omega))
,
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\alpha,i^{\prime}\gamma)=i^{\prime}\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(\alpha,\gamma))
and
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(i\beta,i^{\prime}\gamma)=i^{\prime}\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(\alpha,i^{\prime}\gamma))
, all of which vanish in
I/IJ
. Since
\omega
and
\gamma
vanish in homology, we get that
\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(\beta,\omega))=0
. An analogous method proves that the other two
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}
terms vanish.
∎

An immediate consequence of (
2
) of
3.12
is that we have a well-defined form

(3.2)

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}^{X}\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\times H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to I/IJ,

using the exact sequence from
Appendix

A
. We now aim to show that this form is actually the zero form.

Proposition 3.13
.

The map
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}^{X}\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\times H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to I/IJ
from (
3.2
) is zero.

Remark 3.14
.

An immediate consequence of
3.13
is that
