{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}^{X}:H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\times\im(h_{2}^{\mathbb{Z}/2})\to I/IJ

is well-defined, i.e. for
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
and
c\in\pi_{2}(X)
,
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\mathfrak{f}(z),c)
only depends on the homology class
[c]\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

We postpone the proof of this Proposition until we have established the following lemma which might be of independent interest.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
V_{k,\ell}:=\ker(h_{2}^{\Lambda})\subset\pi_{2}(X\#k\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\ell\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
for non-negative integers
k,\ell
. Then the map induced by collapsing the extra connected-summands

f_{\text{coll}}\colon V_{k,\ell}\to V_{0,0}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We show the result for
k=1,\ell=0
; the general case is a straightforward induction.

Note first that the map
f_{\text{coll}}
is surjective. Since
\pi_{2}(X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2})\cong\pi_{2}(X)\oplus\Lambda
we get that each
a\in V_{1,0}
can be written as a sum
a=b+c
,
b\in V_{0,0}
,
c=\gamma[\mathbb{CP}^{1}]
for
\gamma\in\Lambda
, such that
\varepsilon_{2}(\gamma)=0
. The element
a\otimes 1\in\pi_{2}(X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
satisfies

a\otimes 1=b\otimes 1+\gamma[\mathbb{CP}^{1}]\otimes 1=b\otimes 1+[\mathbb{CP}^{1}]\otimes\varepsilon_{2}(\gamma)=b\otimes 1,

which proves the injectivity of
f_{\text{coll}}
.
∎

Lemma 3.16
.

There are integers
k,\ell
such that there are two
2
-complexes
K_{1},K_{2}

disjointly
mapped into
X\#_{k}\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#_{l}\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
, such that under the collapse map
X\#_{k}\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#_{\ell}\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}\to X
the
2
-complexes
K_{1},K_{2}
map to the
2
-skeleton of
X
.

Proof.

Let
K_{1}\hookrightarrow X
be any
2
-skeleton of
X
. Let
K_{2}^{(1)}
be a wedge of circles embedded in
X
disjoint to
K_{1}
. To make
K_{2}^{(1)}
into a
2
-skeleton, we need to attach some number of
2
-cells to
K_{2}^{(1)}
. We can attach each such 2-cell such that it intersects
K_{1}
in a finite number of points in the
2
-cells of
K_{1}
. For each such intersection we attach
\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}\cong S^{2}\times S^{2}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}
and tube the intersection into the
S^{2}\times S^{2}
to get rid of it (i.e. perform the Norman trick
[
23
]
). Iterating this process over all
2
-cells finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of
3.13
.

Let
k,\ell
be integers and
K_{1},K_{2}
be two complexes as in
Lemma

3.16
. Let
X_{k,\ell}:=X\#k\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\ell\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
and let
a,b\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. From the map of fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{K_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X_{k,\ell}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k,\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}

we get a diagram involving the maps introduced in
Appendix

A

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(K_{1})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(X_{k,\ell})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(K_{2})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{K_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{X_{k,\ell}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{K_{2}}}

The above diagram together with
Lemma

3.16
shows that in
\pi_{2}(X_{k,\ell})
we can make
\mathfrak{f}^{X_{k,\ell}}(a)
and
\mathfrak{f}^{X_{k,\ell}}(b)
disjoint, and so

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}^{X\#k\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\ell\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}}(a,b)=0.

Now the collapse map
X\#k\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\ell\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}\to X
induces an isomorphism on the kernels of the Hurewicz homomorphisms
h_{2}^{\Lambda}
(
Lemma

3.15
) and thus on the images of
\mathfrak{f}^{X\#k\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\ell\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}}
and
\mathfrak{f}^{X}
, which shows that
\mathfrak{f}^{X\#k\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\ell\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}}(a)
and
\mathfrak{f}^{X\#k\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\ell\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}}(b)
can both be represented by spheres in
X
and so
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}^{X}(a,b)={\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}^{X\#k\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\ell\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}}(a,b)=0
.
∎

3.4.
Secondary intersections

Since we will need it in
Lemma

6.3
, we briefly review the Kervaire-Milnor invariant. The Kervaire-Milnor invariant was originally defined in
[
7
, Definition 10.8A]
, generalising the embedding obstruction first investigated in
[
14
]
(see also
[
29
]
). For the history of this secondary intersection invariant we direct the reader to
[
11
, Section 3]
where it is given a full treatment.

Definition 3.17
.

[
11
, Definition 1.4]

Let
b\subset X
be an immersed sphere in a smooth 4-manifold
X
and assume that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(b)=0\in I
, the augmentation ideal of
\Lambda
. Then the
Kervaire-Milnor invariant

\km(b)\in\mathbb{Z}/2
is zero if and only if there exists a collection of Whitney discs
D_{i}
pairing the double points of
b
such that the interiors of the
D_{i}
are all disjoint from
b
, after potentially changing
b
by a regular homotopy.

For the statement of the following theorem the reader should recall
Definition

2.12
of an s-characteristic class and r-characteristic class.

Theorem 3.18
.

[
26
]
[
11
, Theorem 1.6]

Let
b
be an
s
-characteristic immersed sphere in a smooth 4-manifold
X
and assume that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(b)=0
, and hence denote the collection of Whitney discs pairing up the double points of
b
by
D_{i}
. Further assume that
b
has a (potentially unframed) algebraic dual sphere. If
b
is
r
-characteristic then

\km(b)\equiv\sum_{i}|\interior D_{i}\cap b|\pmod{2},

otherwise
\km(b)=0
.

We now demonstrate that the Kervaire-Milnor invariant is the stable obstruction to changing an immersed sphere
b
by regular homotopy to an embedding.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
b
be an immersed sphere in a 4-manifold
X
with
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(b)=0
and
\km(b)=0
. Then
b
is regularly homotopic to an embedding in
X\#k(S^{2}\times S^{2})
for some
k\geq 0
, i.e.
b
is stably regularly homotopic to an embedding.

Proof.

By
Definition

3.17
, we can choose a collection of framed Whitney discs
D_{i}
for
b
whose interiors are disjoint from
b
. We wish to perform Whitney moves over all of the
D_{i}
such that
b
becomes embedded, but to do this we need to remove all of the intersections between the various Whitney discs. Let
p\in D_{i}\cap D_{j}
. Form the connected-sum
X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})
and tube
D_{i}
into
S^{2}\times\{\pt\}\subset S^{2}\times S^{2}
(the standard Norman trick
[
23
]
). Note that this operation preserves the discs being framed. Now
D_{i}
has a geometric dual sphere
\{\pt\}\times S^{2}
, and so we can remove the intersection point
p
by tubing
D_{j}
into the geometric dual (again this preserves the framing). Repeating this process for all intersection points makes all of the Whitney discs disjointly embedded and framed; hence performing the corresponding Whitney moves takes
b
to an embedding.
∎

We give an example, which will be used in
Lemma

6.3
, of an immersed sphere with non-trivial Kervaire-Milnor invariant, which is due to
[
14
]
.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
w\in H_{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{2})
be a generator. Then the class
3w
can be represented by an immersed sphere
b
with
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(b)=0
and
\km(b)=1
.

Proof.

By adding trivial cusps, it is clear that one can arrange for an immersion
b
with
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(b)=0
representing
3w
. One can verify that the argument given in
[
14
, Example 2]
works even if we allow stabilisations (stabilisations do not change the signature), and hence
3w
cannot be represented by an embedding, even stably. Hence,
\km(b)=1
.
∎

4.
Obstruction theory for filling 3-manifolds

4.1.
The primary obstruction

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type and let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-3-manifold. Recall (
Definition

2.6
) that we have the natural map
\xi\to\xi^{\prime}:=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},\infty)
and by a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
of
Y
we mean a null-bordism of
Y
in
\Omega_{3}^{\xi}
. In other words
X
is a
\xi^{\prime}
-manifold with boundary
Y
, whose
\xi^{\prime}
-structure agrees with the
\xi
structure on the boundary.

Recall that for a
\xi
-3-manifold
(Y,u_{Y})
there is a well-defined primary obstruction
\pri(Y,u_{Y})\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})
. The following is a well-known result.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type where we allow the totally non-spin case, i.e.
w_{2}^{\pi}\in H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cup\{\infty\}
. Let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-
3
-manifold. Then the primary obstruction
\pri(Y,u_{Y})
equals
(u_{Y})_{*}[Y]\in\ker d_{2}\subset H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})
, where
[Y]\in H_{3}(Y;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(Y)})
is the twisted fundamental class and
d_{2}
denotes the appropriate second differential in the James spectral sequence.

For the proof see, for example,
[
27
, Proposition 3.2.1]
which can be readily adapted for the non-orientable case.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type. Let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-3-manifold. Then
\pri(Y,u_{Y})=0
if and only if there if a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling
(X,u_{X}^{\pi}\colon X\to B\pi)
of
(Y,u_{Y})
such that
u_{X}^{\pi}
induces an isomorphism on the fundamental groups.

Proof.

The proof is by comparison
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})\to\xi^{\prime}=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},\infty)
. Naturality of the James spectral sequence gives a diagram of edge homomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{3}^{\xi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{3}^{\xi^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the vertical map is an isomorphism since the group
\Omega_{*}^{\xi^{\prime}}
can be calculated using the James spectral sequence

H_{p}(\pi;(\Omega_{3}^{SO})^{w_{1}^{\pi}})\Rightarrow\Omega_{p+q}^{\xi^{\prime}},

and the bordism group
\Omega_{*}^{SO}
vanishes in dimensions
1,2
and
3
.

This shows that
\pri(Y,u_{Y})=0
if and only if
Y
vanishes in
\Omega_{3}^{\xi^{\prime}}
and we finish the proof by applying
Lemma

2.7
.
∎

4.2.
The secondary obstruction

Let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
3
-dimensional
\xi
-manifold. Assume that
\pri(Y)
vanishes and choose a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
. Recall that
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the obstruction to extending the
\xi
-structure over
X
rel
Y
(see
Proposition

2.8
). We denote by
w\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
the Poincaré dual of
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
. Using the James spectral sequence for
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
, recall that there is now a well-defined secondary invariant
\secondary(Y,u_{Y})\in H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2})
(there is no outgoing differential, see
Proposition

B.9
). We now define the secondary invariant geometrically using
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
. Later in
Section

5
we will quotient out by the choice of this filling to get a well-defined invariant that will be equal to
\secondary(Y,u_{Y})
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type, let
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
be a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling of a
\xi
-3-manifold
(Y,u_{Y})
, and let
w\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
be the obstruction class as above. Then we define the
geometric secondary obstruction
to be

\secG(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi}):=(u_{X}^{\pi})_{*}(w)\in H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2).

In the above definition if
Y
is empty we denote
\secG(X,\emptyset,\emptyset,u_{X}^{\pi})=\secG(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal 1-type, let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-3-manifold with
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling and
u_{X}^{\pi}
inducing an isomorphism on fundamental groups. Then the Poincaré dual of
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{X}^{\pi},u_{Y})
is spherical if and only if
\secG(X,Y,u_{X}^{\pi},u_{Y})=0
.

Proof.

The result follows from considering the Serre exact sequence and the surjectivity of the map
\pi_{2}(X)\to\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
.
∎

4.3.
The tertiary obstruction

We now use the self-intersection invariant
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}\colon\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}/2\to I/IJ\cong H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
from
Section

3
to define a geometric tertiary obstruction
\terG
.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type, let
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
be a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling of a
\xi
-3-manifold
(Y,u_{Y})
such that
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is a spherical homology class, i.e.
\secG(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})=0
. Let
c\in\pi_{2}(X)
satisfy
h_{2}(c)=\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
, where
h_{2}
denotes the
\mathbb{Z}/2
Hurewicz map. Then we define the
geometric tertiary obstruction
to be

\terG(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi},c):=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2).

This value depends on the bounding manifold
X
as well as the choice of
c
representing
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
. To get rid of these dependencies we have to take an appropriate quotient. We will show how to do this in the next section.

5.
Differentials and well-definedness of the obstructions

5.1.
The geometric differential
\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}

We now define the geometric differential

\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}\colon H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})\to H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)

for the secondary invariant which was briefly described in
Section

1
. We then show that it is equal to the corresponding
d_{2}
differential in the James spectral sequence. We start with the definition.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
x\in H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})
be any element. Represent
x
as a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
with
u_{M}^{\pi}
a
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism (its existence follows from the proof of
4.2
). Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal 1-type and let
w\in H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
be Poincaré dual to
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
. We define

\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}(x):=\secG(M,u_{M}^{\pi}).

It is currently not clear that the above definition is well-defined, i.e. does not depend on the choice of representative manifold. We deal with this now by showing that
\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}
is
equal
to
d_{2}^{(4,0)}
from the JSS. Since the latter is already well-defined, it follows that
\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}
is also.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal 1-type, let
(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
be a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold and let
x=(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}[M]
be the image of the twisted fundamental class
[M]\in H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(M)})
. Then
\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}(x)=d_{2}^{(4,0)}(x)
.

Proof.

We will prove the following string of equalities.

d_{2}^{(4,0)}(x)=(u^{\pi}_{M})_{*}(PD(w_{2}(M))+PD((u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi}))=(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}(PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi})))=\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}(x).

The second equality follows from
Proposition

2.8
, and the third is by
Definition

5.1
. We now concern ourselves with the first equality.

The Universal coefficient theorem gives an isomorphism
H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\Hom(H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2),\mathbb{Z}/2)
and hence the first equality of the above holds if and only if

\displaystyle\langle w_{2}^{\pi}\smile\alpha+w_{1}^{\pi}\smile\Sq^{1}(\alpha)+

\displaystyle\Sq^{2}(\alpha),(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}[M]\rangle=

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha,(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}(w_{2}(M)\frown[M])\rangle+\langle\alpha,(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}((u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}w_{2}^{\pi}\frown[M])\rangle

for all
\alpha\in H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
(above we have used the description of the second differential from
Proposition

B.9
).

We calculate

\displaystyle\langle

\displaystyle w_{2}^{\pi}\smile\alpha+w_{1}^{\pi}\smile\Sq^{1}(\alpha)+\Sq^{2}(\alpha),(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}[M]\rangle=

\displaystyle=\langle w_{2}^{\pi}\smile\alpha,(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}[M]\rangle+\langle w_{1}^{\pi}\smile\Sq^{1}(\alpha)\rangle+\langle\alpha^{2},(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}[M]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}\alpha,(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}w_{2}^{\pi}\frown[M]\rangle+\langle(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(w_{1}^{\pi})\smile\Sq^{1}((u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(\alpha)),[M]\rangle+\langle(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(\alpha)^{2},[M]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha,(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}((u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}w_{2}^{\pi}\frown[M])\rangle+\langle w_{1}(M)\smile\Sq^{1}((u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(\alpha))\rangle+\langle(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(\alpha)\smile w_{2}(M),[M]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha,(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}((u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}w_{2}^{\pi}\frown[M])\rangle+\langle\Sq^{1}(\Sq^{1}((u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(\alpha)))[M]\rangle+\langle(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(\alpha),w_{2}(M)\frown[M]\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha,(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}((u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}w_{2}^{\pi}\frown[M])\rangle+\langle\alpha,(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}(w_{2}(M)\frown[M])\rangle.

Here the first equality follows by linearity of the Kronecker pairing, the second by the cup-cap formula, and the third equality used that the normal second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}
equals the second tangential Wu class
v^{t}_{2}
and so acts a characteristic element.
2
2

2

In general we have for tangential Wu classes of
n
-dimensional manifolds
W
that

v_{i}^{t}x=\Sq^{i}(x)
for
x\in H^{n-i}(W;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
The fourth equality follows from
w_{1}=w_{1}^{t}=v_{1}^{t}
, and the last equality uses the Adem relation
\Sq^{1}\Sq^{1}=0
.
∎

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal 1-type. Let
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
be a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
, with
\pi_{1}(u_{X}^{\pi})
an isomorphism. Let
w\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
be Poincaré-Lefschetz dual to the obstruction class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
. Then

H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im d_{2}^{(4,0)}\ni\secondary(Y,u_{Y})=[\secG(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})]\in H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}

and hence
[\secG(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})]\in H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}
does not depend on the choice of
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling.

Proof.

This is due to the second-named author
[
27
, Proposition 3.2.3]
.
∎

We now finish this subsection by showing the following improvement of
4.4
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal 1-type. Let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-3-manifold with
\pri(Y,u_{Y})=0
. Then there exists
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling of
Y
with the Poincaré-Lefschetz dual to
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
spherical if and only if
\secondary(Y,u_{Y})=0
.

Proof.

The ‘only if’ direction follows from
4.4
.

Conversely, assume
\secondary(Y,u_{Y})=0
. By
4.1
and
5.3
there exists a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling
(X^{\prime},u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi})
of
(Y,u_{Y})
such that
\secG(X^{\prime},Y,u_{Y},u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi})
is in the image of
\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}
. This implies that there exists a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
such that

\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}(u_{M*}^{\pi}([M]))=\secG(X^{\prime},Y,u_{Y},u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi}).

Form the connected sum
X^{\prime\prime}:=M\#X^{\prime}
and
u^{\pi}_{X^{\prime\prime}}:=(u_{M}^{\pi}\#u_{X}^{\pi})\colon M\#X^{\prime}\to B\pi
. By
Lemma

2.11
the Poincaré dual of
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X^{\prime\prime},Y,u_{Y},u_{X^{\prime\prime}}^{\pi})
can be written as a sum
w_{X^{\prime\prime}}=w_{M}+w_{X^{\prime}}
of the Poincaré duals of the obstructions of the summands. We have

u_{M*}^{\pi}(w_{M})+(u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi})_{*}(w_{X^{\prime}})=0\in H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2),

Realise
w_{X^{\prime\prime}}
by some surface
\Sigma
in
X^{\prime\prime}
. Using
Lemma

2.7
we can surger
X^{\prime\prime}
to turn the classifying map
u^{\pi}_{X^{\prime\prime}}
to a
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism, while preserving
\xi
structure in the complement of
\Sigma
. Call the resulting
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
manifold
X
. Now
\Sigma
is a surface in
X
representing
\secG(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})=0
. Since
u_{X}^{\pi}(\Sigma)=0
, applying
4.4
implies that the class
[\Sigma]
is spherical.
∎

5.2.
The geometric differential
\delta_{2}^{(3,1)}

We now set out to define the geometric differential
\delta_{2}^{(3,1)}
for the tertiary invariant that was briefly described in
Section

1
. We then show that it is equal to the corresponding
d_{2}
differential in the JSS. Due to an abundance of superscripts, we will drop the
(3,1)
superscript in this section, and so the unaltered differential will simply read as
\delta_{2}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type, let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a (possibly empty)
\xi
-
3
-manifold, and let
X
be its
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling. Let
c\in\pi_{2}(X)
be s-characteristic and let
w:=[c]\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Then we have the following

(1)

The map

\delta_{2}^{X,c}:=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(-)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(-,c):\ker(h_{2}\colon\pi_{2}(X)\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2))\to I/IJ

only depends on
[c]\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, and we denote the map above as
\delta_{2}^{X,w}
.

(2)

The map
\delta_{2}^{X,w}
factors as

\delta_{2}^{X,w}=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(-)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(-,w)\colon\ker(h_{2}^{\Lambda}\colon\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\to H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2))\to I/IJ,

and so the following map is well-defined

\delta_{2}^{X,w}=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(-)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(-,w)\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to I/IJ.

(3)

The map
\delta_{2}^{X,w}\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to I/IJ
only depends on the
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
-stable equivalence class of the manifold
X
, i.e.

\delta_{2}^{X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2},w+[\mathbb{CP}^{1}]}=\delta_{2}^{X,w}=\delta_{2}^{X\#\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}},w+[\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{1}}]}.

Proof.

(
1
) For a fixed homology class
w\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, different lifts
c\in\pi_{2}(X)
differ by elements
\beta\in\pi_{2}(X)
such that
h_{2}(\beta)=0
. Let
\alpha\in\ker(h_{2})\subset\pi_{2}(X)
.

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\alpha)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\alpha,c+\beta)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\alpha)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\alpha,c)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\alpha,\beta)

Now from
3.13
we get
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\alpha,\beta)=0
.

For (
2
) let
\alpha\in\ker(h_{2}^{\Lambda})\subset\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
and let
\overline{\alpha}\in\pi_{2}(X)
be any lift. Note that a different lift differs by
j\beta\in J\ker(h_{2}^{\Lambda})
by
Lemma

3.6
(
2
). Observe that

\displaystyle\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\overline{\alpha}+j\beta)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\overline{\alpha}+

\displaystyle j\beta,c)=

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\overline{\alpha})+(j-\varepsilon(j))(\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(\beta,\beta))+\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(\beta,\overline{\alpha})))+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\overline{\alpha},c)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(j\beta,c)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\overline{\alpha})+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\overline{\alpha},c)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(j\beta,c)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\overline{\alpha})+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\overline{\alpha},c)+(j-\varepsilon(j))\varepsilon_{2}\lambda(\beta,c)

\displaystyle=\mu_{J}(\overline{\alpha})+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\overline{\alpha},c),

where we used
Lemma

3.11
(
4
) and (
5
) and the fact that
\varepsilon_{2}(\lambda(-,-))
is zero if at least one of the arguments is null-homologous.

For (
3
), we do the proof for
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
. The proof for
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
is analogous. Let
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. We use the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}^{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}^{\Lambda}}

induced by the collapse map
X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}\to X
. From
Lemma

3.15
we find that the middle vertical map induces an isomorphism on the images of
\mathfrak{f}^{X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
and
\mathfrak{f}^{X}
. It follows that
\mathfrak{f}^{X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}}(z)
can be represented by a sphere in
X
, which proves the claim.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type, let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a (possibly empty)
\xi
-
3
-manifold, let
X
be its
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling and assume the obstruction class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
is spherical. Then there exists a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
, with
\pi_{1}(u_{M}^{\pi})
an isomorphism,
u_{M*}^{\pi}[M]=0
for the twisted fundamental class
[M]\in H_{4}(M;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})
and
w_{M}:=\PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi}))
is spherical such that

\delta_{2}^{X,w_{X}}=\delta_{2}^{M,w_{M}}\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2),

where
w_{X}
is the Poincaré dual of
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
.

Proof.

If
X
is already closed, work instead with punctured
X
. Now assume
X
is a compact 4-manifold with non-empty boundary. Consider the inclusion into the double
i\colon X\hookrightarrow DX
and the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(DX)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{DX}}

Let
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
Lemma

2.11
implies that the obstruction to a
\xi
-structure on
DX
is given by
i_{*}w_{X}+Dw_{X}\in H_{2}(DX;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, where
Dw_{X}
is the image of
w_{X}
in the other half of
DX
. The commutative diagram above shows that the image of
a
in
\pi_{2}(DX)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
can be realised by a sphere entirely in
X
. It immediately follows that

\displaystyle\delta_{2}^{DX,i_{*}w+Dw}(z)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}^{DX}(\mathfrak{f}^{X}(z))+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}^{DX}_{J}(\mathfrak{f}^{X}(z),i_{*}w+Dw)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}^{X}(\mathfrak{f}^{X}(z))+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}^{X}(\mathfrak{f}^{X}(z),i_{*}w)=\delta_{2}^{X,w}(z).\qed

The proof of the following proposition will comprise the next ten pages.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type, let
X
be a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-
4
-manifold with possibly non-empty boundary, a
\xi
-3-manifold
(Y,u_{Y})
. Let
w\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
be the Poincaré dual of the obstruction class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Then we have that
\delta_{2}^{X,w}\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to I/IJ
defined in
Proposition

5.5
(
2
) satisfies

\delta^{X,w}_{2}=d_{2}\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2).

In particular, it does not depend on the choice of manifold
X
(only on the normal 1-type
\xi
).

We will first solve the
\Spin
and
\Pin^{+}
case of
Proposition

5.7
, which we will then use to solve the general case. Recall that for
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},0)
the differential
d_{2}\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the Hom dual of
\Sq^{1}(-)\smile w_{1}
, since the second Steenrod square vanishes in this case (see
Appendix

B
).

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},0)
be a
\Spin
or
\Pin^{+}
normal
1
-type (depending on whether
w_{1}^{\pi}
is zero or not). Let
(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
be a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold with
\secG(M,u_{M}^{\pi})=0
. Then

\delta_{2}^{M,w}=(w_{1}^{\pi}\smile\Sq^{1}(-))^{*}=d_{2}\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2).

The proof of
Proposition

5.8
relies on first proving it in a few special cases that we will then compare to.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let

(i)

\pi=\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\xi=\xi(\pi,0,0)
, or

(ii)

\pi=\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\xi=\xi(\pi,x_{1},0)
for
x_{1}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
the generator, or

(iii)

\pi=\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\xi=\xi(\pi,x_{1}+x_{2},0)
for
x_{1},x_{2}
the standard generators of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

Then for a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
u_{M}^{\pi}\colon M\to B\pi
, with
\secG(M,u_{M}^{\pi})=0
the conclusion of
Proposition

5.8
holds.

We will use the following construction multiple times during the proof, so we record it here.

Construction 5.10
.

For a manifold
M
with
\pi_{1}(M)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2*\mathbb{Z}/2
we denote by
(M)_{*}
the result of a surgery on the commutator of the generators of
\pi_{1}(M)
. If
M
has a normal
\Pin^{+}
structure away from some characteristic surface
w
this surgery can be preformed disjointly from
w
and in a
\Pin^{+}
-preserving way away from
w
.

Proof of
Lemma

5.9
.

By
Proposition

5.5
(
3
) we only need to consider each
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
-stable class
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
. By
[
18
]
there is
|H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})/\Aut(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})|
many
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
-stable classes
3
3

3

Allowing connected summing with both
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
and
\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
.
of manifolds with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation class
w_{1}^{\pi}
. The secondary invariant does not depend on the stable
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
-class.

Case (i),
\xi=\xi(\mathbb{Z}/2,0,0)
: There is exactly
|H_{4}(B\mathbb{Z}/2)/\Aut(\mathbb{Z}/2)|=1
such stable class, represented by a manifold commonly denoted by
F
; this is a closed spin manifold with
\pi_{1}(F)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
and universal cover
S^{2}\times S^{2}
. We will use some topological properties of
F
listed for example in
[
9
, End of Section 4]
. The deck transformation on the universal cover of
F
is

\displaystyle S^{2}\times S^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow S^{2}\times S^{2}

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto(-x,r(y)),

The
k
-invariant
k_{F}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\pi_{2}(F))
is
(1,0)
under the obvious isomorphism
\pi_{2}(S^{2}\times S^{2})\cong\pi_{2}(S^{2})\times\pi_{2}(S^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}\left<a,b\right>
. By
A.6
, the evaluation map of the reduced
k
-invariant
k^{r}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\pi_{2}(F)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z})
give the first map of the Serre exact sequence for
F
(see
Appendix

A
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(F)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(F;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev(k_{F})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}^{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1,0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj_{2}}

Since
F
is Spin we need to check that
\delta_{2}^{F,0}(\alpha)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\alpha)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{j}(\alpha,0)
is zero for any
\alpha\in\ker(h_{2}^{\Lambda})
. The kernel
\ker(h_{2}^{\Lambda})
is generated by the class
\alpha=[*\times S^{2}]
. From the deck transformation we see that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\alpha)=0
and so
\delta_{2}^{F,0}=0
. The expression
(w_{1}^{\pi}\smile\Sq^{1}(-))^{*}
vanishes as well.

Case (ii),
\xi=\xi(\mathbb{Z}/2,w_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}=x_{1},0)
: Since
H_{4}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
we need to consider two stable
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
-classes. These are
\mathbb{RP}^{4}
and
\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}
for the non-trivial and the trivial-class respectively, as can be seen from their classifying maps.

The obstruction to these manifolds being
normally

\Pin^{+}
equals the obstruction to them being tangentially
\Pin^{-}
. Since
w^{t}_{1}(\mathbb{RP}^{4})^{2}+w^{t}_{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{4})=x_{1}^{2}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, its Poincaré dual represents the non-trivial class in
H_{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Since the classifying map
u_{\mathbb{RP}^{4}}^{\pi}\colon\mathbb{RP}^{4}\to B\mathbb{Z}/2\simeq\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
is the inclusion the secondary invariant
\secG(\mathbb{RP}^{4},u_{\mathbb{RP}^{4}}^{\pi})
is non-zero.

On the other hand, the manifold
\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}
is normally
\Pin^{+}
and so the obstruction class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2},u_{\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}}^{\pi})
can be represented by a null homologous element and so
\secG(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2},u_{\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}}^{\pi})
vanishes. To show that
\delta_{2}^{\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2},0}=(x_{1}\smile\Sq^{1}(-))^{*}
we first calculate the right-hand side. The map
H^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
given by
x_{1}\smile\Sq^{1}(-)
is an isomorphism and so is its dual.
To calculate the left-hand side we need to examine the relevant portion of the Serre exact sequence, which is

H_{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}^{\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}}}\pi_{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2})\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{h_{2}^{\Lambda}}H_{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}).

The kernel of the second map is
\mathbb{Z}/2
generated by the universal cover
a\colon S^{2}\to\mathbb{RP}^{2}
postcomposed with the inclusion
\iota_{\mathbb{RP}^{2}}\colon\mathbb{RP}^{2}\to\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}
. We show that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\iota_{\mathbb{RP}^{2}}\circ a)\in H_{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the nontrivial element as follows. Let
f_{\text{fold}}\colon S^{2}\to D^{2}
denote the fold map which sends the equator of
S^{2}
into the boundary of
D^{2}
and the north and south-pole to
0\in D^{2}
. The following map is homotopic to
\iota_{\mathbb{RP}^{2}}\circ a
.

(5.1)

a^{\prime}\colon S^{2}\xrightarrow{\id_{S^{2}}\times f_{\text{fold}}}S^{2}\times D^{2}\xrightarrow{a\times\id_{D^{2}}}\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times D^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}

The image of this map has a single double point, namely it sends the south-pole and the north-pole to the same element. Since the line which joins the north-pole to the south-pole is the generator of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{RP}^{2})
we conclude that the double-point loop associated to this intersection is the generator of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2})
and hence
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a^{\prime})=x_{1}\neq 0
.

Case (iii),
\xi=\xi(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2,w_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2}=x_{1}+x_{2},0)
:

We use the model
B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2)\simeq B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2\simeq\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
to calculate
H_{4}(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}^{x_{1}+x_{2}})
. The universal cover of
\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
is the contractible space
S^{\infty}
. The singular chain complex for
S^{\infty}
is
C_{i}(S^{\infty})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]
with the differential
d_{i}:C_{i}(S^{\infty})\to C_{i-1}(S^{\infty})
given by
t+(-1)^{i}
which can be used to calculate that

H_{4}(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}^{x_{1}+x_{2}})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{3}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\left<e_{0}\times e_{4},e_{2}\times e_{2},e_{4}\times e_{0}\right>

and

H_{4}(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}^{x_{1}+x_{2}})/\Aut_{x_{1}+x_{2}}(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\left<e_{0}\times e_{4},e_{2}\times e_{2}\right>,

Where we take the cellular decomposition of
S^{\infty}
to have two cells in each dimension and the universal covering projection
S^{\infty}\to\mathbb{RP}^{2}
to be cellular.
We now aim to find manifolds to represent the classes
\left<e_{0}\times e_{4},e_{2}\times e_{2}\right>
. The class
e_{2}\times e_{2}
is given by
\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}
. The class
e_{0}\times e_{4}
is given by
(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#\mathbb{RP}^{4})_{*}
, since this class is given by
S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#\mathbb{RP}^{4}\to B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2
and cobordism preserves this image. Finally, the zero class is given by
(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2})_{*}
. For all of the above manifolds we use the obvious map to
\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
. Since these computations will be lengthy, we split into sub cases.

Case (iii.a),
X=((S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2})\#(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}))_{*}
: The Kirby diagram of
X
is given in
Figure

1
. Here the secondary invariant of
X
vanishes, since
X
is normally
\Pin^{+}
and so the obstruction class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
vanishes.

Figure 1.
A Kirby diagram for the manifold
X=((\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2})\#(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}))_{*}
. The notation for the non-orientable 1-handles is due to Akbulut
[
1
, Sections 1.1, 1.5]

We will define some elements
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
that we will need later. Let
C\cong D^{2}
be the core of the
S^{2}\times D^{2}
which was attached during the surgery to form
X
. We define annuli
A_{t},A_{s}\colon S^{1}\times D^{2}\hookrightarrow X
such that each boundary of the annuli maps to
\partial C
, and define
\alpha_{1}=C\cup A_{t}\cup\overline{C}
and
\alpha_{2}=C\cup A_{s}\cup\overline{C}
. We describe the annulus
A_{t}
using an inertial regular homotopy of a push-off
\gamma
of

(see
Figure

2
). The annulus
A_{s}
is defined analogously.

Now we make the following claim about the properties of
X
that we will need.

Claim 5.11
.

Let
X=((\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2})\#(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}))_{*}
and let
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
. Then the Serre exact sequence (Appendix
A
) sequence associated to

is

(5.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}

Then the following holds. The groups involved are given by

(1)

H_{k}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/2\ \text{if}\ k=0,4\\

(2)

and

.

We collect information about the generators:

(3)

The group

(4)

and

(5)

The group

, the two
\{\pt\}\times S^{2}
and the belt sphere to the surgery, as well as two classes

(6)

, the belt sphere to the surgery, as well as another class
R
.

and the maps:

on

(8)

This finishes the statement of the claim. We delay its proof until after we finish the current proof.

Figure 2.
The annulus
A_{t}
described using a regular homotopy of

, shown in sporadically dashed green, whereas the relevant parts of the Kirby diagram are shown in solid black. The guide for the single intersection point is shown in dashed purple. The handle slides are guided by the blue arrows. Only relevant portions of the Kirby diagram from

Since
w=\PD(X,u_{X}^{\pi})=0
, we have that
\delta_{2}^{X,0}=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}\circ\mathfrak{f}^{X}
. By
5.11
(
8
) we need to calculate the self intersection of elements in the image of
\mathfrak{f}^{X}
. One can compute from
Figure

2
that
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
have a single double-point and furthermore that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\alpha_{1})=t
and
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\alpha_{2})=s
, where
s,t\in H_{1}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
are generators dual to
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
respectively. The two covering maps
S^{2}\to\mathbb{RP}^{2}
have
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}=t
and
s
, respectively (the proof is identical to the earlier calculation for
\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times S^{2}
in this proof, see map
5.1
).

The map
d_{2}
is dual to
(w_{1}^{\pi}\smile\Sq^{1}(-))\colon H^{1}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{3}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, which is given on the generators by

\displaystyle x_{1}\mapsto x_{1}^{3}+x_{1}^{2}x_{2}

\displaystyle x_{2}\mapsto x_{1}x_{2}^{2}+x_{2}^{3}

since
w_{1}^{\pi}=x_{1}+x_{2}
. Now
d_{2}
is

\displaystyle x_{1}^{3}\mapsto t=(x_{1})^{*}

\displaystyle x_{1}^{2}x_{2}\mapsto t=(x_{1})^{*}

\displaystyle x_{2}^{3}\mapsto s=(x_{2})^{*}

\displaystyle x_{1}x_{2}^{2}\mapsto s=(x_{2})^{*}

which agrees with
\delta_{2}^{X,0}
.

Case (iii, b), the manifold
X=\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}
is
not
normally
\Pin^{+}
and the normal
w_{2}
is given by
x_{1}x_{2}
. This element does not vanish in
H_{2}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)
and so

\secG(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)=x_{1}x_{2}\neq 0.

Case (iii, c),
X=((S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2})\#\mathbb{RP}^{4})_{*}
: A calculation shows that
S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#\mathbb{RP}^{4}
has the normal second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}=x_{2}^{2}
. Let
w\in H_{2}(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#\mathbb{RP}^{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)
be the Poincaré dual of
x_{2}^{2}
. By preforming surgery like in
5.10
we obtain
(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#\mathbb{RP}^{4})_{*}
, which still has
w
as a characteristic element. Furthermore, an easy Mayer-Vietoris argument shows that the class
w
is still-nontrivial and we obtain the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#\mathbb{RP}^{4};\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(B(\mathbb{Z}/2*\mathbb{Z}/2);\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}((S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#\mathbb{RP}^{4})_{*};\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2);\mathbb{Z}/2)}

The left vertical map is actually given by a zigzag of Mayer-Vietoris sequences. The commutativity of the above diagram then gives that
\sec
is non-zero, since
w
maps non-trivially into
H_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
∎

Proof of
5.11
.

We first show (
1
) Calculating the (generators for the) homology of
B(\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2)\simeq\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
is a basic exercise in algebraic topology, proving (
3
) and (
4
).

Let
X_{0}
obtained by removing the attaching region of the surgery

(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2})\#(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2})\rightsquigarrow(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2})_{*}=:X,

which was performed away from some fixed representative of
w
. Then
X_{0}
admits inclusions
S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\hookleftarrow X_{0}\hookrightarrow X
.

Consider the following Mayer-Vietoris sequence for the first inclusion (with
\mathbb{Z}/2
-coefficients omitted).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(S^{1}\times S^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
belt

The last map is zero because the next map is an injection
H_{1}(S^{1}\times S^{2})\to H_{1}(S^{1}\times D^{2})\oplus H_{1}(X_{0})
. The image of the map labelled “belt” gives a class in
X_{0}
and in turn in
X
is given by the belt sphere of the handle. We now state the corresponding sequence for the second inclusion.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(S^{1}\times S^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(X_{0})\oplus H_{2}(D^{2}\times S^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(S^{1}\times S^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Here the last map is zero because the generator of
H_{1}(S^{1}\times S^{2})
maps to a commutator in
X_{0}
and hence vanishes in homology.

Via the two sequences above we conclude that we have a (non-canonical) isomorphism
H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{6}\cong H_{2}(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}
where the
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{2}
class is generated by the belt sphere as defined above and some class
R
and
H_{2}(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{4}
is generated by two copies of
S^{2}
and two copies of
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
. This proves (
1
) and (
6
).

Consider the Serre exact sequence (
5.2
). The last map
(u_{X}^{\pi})_{*}\colon H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is surjective; its kernel is
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{3}
, generated by the two embedded
2
-spheres and the belt sphere of the attached handle, since spherical classes necessarily vanish in
H_{2}(B\pi)
. This proves (
10
). We show that the first map
(u_{X}^{\pi})_{*}
is zero. The map
S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\to B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2
factors through the 2-skeleton
(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2)_{(2)}
and so does the map
X\to B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2
, since, up to homotopy equivalence, only a 2-handle was attached, which proves (
7
).

Since
H_{3}(B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{4}
we have that
\pi_{2}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{7}
, proving (
2
). Since the image of
h_{2}^{\Lambda}
is
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{3}
its kernel must be
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{4}
. To identify the generators, we consider the map
\mathfrak{f}
. By
A.6
, an alternate description of
\mathfrak{f}
is the following: a model for
B\pi
can be constructed by attaching
n
-cells for
n\geq 3
to
X
to kill all higher homotopy groups. In this model any
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is given by a linear combination of 3-cells, which attach along 2-spheres in
X
. Then
\mathfrak{f}(z)
is this linear combination of attaching maps. We use this description now.

Factor the classifying map of
X
as

X=(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}\#S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2})_{*}\to(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2})\vee(S^{2}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2})\cup f_{2}\to\mathbb{RP}^{2}\vee\mathbb{RP}^{2}\cup f_{2}\xrightarrow{\text{incl.}}\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{RP}^{\infty},

where
f_{2}
denotes a 2-cell that attaches along the commutator. Since
\mathbb{RP}^{2}\vee\mathbb{RP}^{2}\cup f_{2}
is precisely the 2-skeleton of
\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
, the map
\mathfrak{f}
is easy to see on that space; it is given by the attaching maps of the 3-cells in
\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
. We obtain the diagram, which commutes by naturality of Serre spectral sequences (see
Appendix

A
).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\vee\mathbb{RP}^{2}\cup f_{2})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{2}}

By the characterization above, the map
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
sends
e_{3}\times e_{0}
to
2e_{2}\times e_{0}
,
e_{0}\times e_{3}
to
e_{0}\times 2e_{2}
,
e_{2}\times e_{1}
to
2e_{1}\times e_{1}\cup e_{2}\times 2e_{0}
, and
e_{1}\times e_{2}
to
2e_{0}\times e_{2}\cup e_{1}\times 2e_{1}
. Note that the two covering maps
(S^{2}\to\mathbb{RP}^{2})\in\pi_{2}(X)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2
map to
2e_{2}\times e_{0}
and
e_{0}\times 2e_{2}
in
\pi_{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\vee\mathbb{RP}^{2}\cup f_{2})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
The maps
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
is an isomorphism and so
\mathfrak{f}_{1}(e_{3}\times e_{0})
and
\mathfrak{f}_{2}(e_{0}\times e_{3})
map to the covering maps, since the covering maps vanish in
\mathbb{Z}/2
-homology and so lift to
H_{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

We now show that
\alpha_{1}=\mathfrak{f}_{1}(e_{2}\times e_{1})
(showing
\alpha_{2}=\mathfrak{f}_{1}(e_{1}\times e_{2})
is the same). Like above, it suffices to show that
\alpha_{1}
maps to
\partial(e_{2}\times e_{1})\in\pi_{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{2}\vee\mathbb{RP}^{2}\cup f_{2})\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2)
. The two discs
C
and
\overline{C}
both map to
f_{2}
, so we only have to conclude that the annulus
A_{1}
maps to
2e_{2}\times e_{0}
. Recall that the annulus was formed by two handle slides, both over the 2-handle whose core maps to
e_{2}\times e_{0}
. This implies that the trace of the annulus maps to
2e_{2}\times e_{0}
, and hence
\alpha_{1}
maps to
\partial(e_{2}\times e_{1})
. This proves (
8
). Using the above and (
10
) we show (
5
). Combining (
5
) and (
6
) proves (
9
).
∎

Proof of
Proposition

5.8
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},0)
be given and let
(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
be a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold with
\secG(M,u_{M}^{\pi})=0
. We show that we can always set up a comparison with one of the 1-types considered in
Lemma

5.9
that will suffice to describe the differential.

Let
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
, let
\overline{\mathfrak{f}^{M}(z)}\colon S^{2}\looparrowright X
be an immersed 2-sphere such that
[\overline{\mathfrak{f}^{M}(z)}]\in\pi_{2}(M)
is a lift of
\mathfrak{f}^{M}(z)
, and let
[c\colon S^{2}\looparrowright M]\in\pi_{2}(M)
be a lift of
\PD(w_{2}(M))
, which exists since the secondary invariant of
M
vanishes. Assume towards a contradiction that
x:=\delta_{2}^{M,w}(z)-d_{2}(z)\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is non-zero. Then there exists a map
\varphi_{x}\colon\pi\to\mathbb{Z}/2
which maps
x
to the generator of
H_{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. We now split into cases.

(1)

If
w_{1}^{\pi}=0
then surger out the kernel of
\varphi_{x}
to form a
(B\mathbb{Z}/2,w_{1}^{\pi}=0)
-manifold
M^{\prime}
.

(2)

If
w_{1}^{\pi}(x)\neq 0
then surger out the kernel of
w_{1}^{\pi}
to form a
(B\mathbb{Z}/2,w_{1}^{\pi}\neq 0)
-manifold
M^{\prime}
.

(3)

If
w_{1}^{\pi}\neq 0
but
w_{1}^{\pi}(x)=0
then surger out a kernel of
w_{1}^{\pi}\oplus\varphi_{x}\colon\pi\to\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2
to form a
(B(\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2),w_{1}^{\pi}=(1,0))
-manifold
M^{\prime}
.

In all cases perform these surgeries away from
\overline{\mathfrak{f}^{M}(z)}
and
c
, which can be done by general position. Denote this
5
-dimensional surgery trace by
W
. The class
c
still represents the second Stiefel-Whitney class of
M^{\prime}
since we may assume that our surgeries preserve
\Spin/\Pin^{+}
-structures in the complement of
c
.

Let
i_{M}\colon M\to W
,
i_{M^{\prime}}\colon M^{\prime}\to W
denote the two inclusions. Consider the following diagram where the rows are formed from the Serre exact sequence for
M
,
W
and
M^{\prime}
, respectively, which commutes by naturality.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(M)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi_{1}(W);\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(W)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi_{1}(M^{\prime});\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(M^{\prime})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(M^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}/2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{M})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{M})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{M})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{M^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{M^{\prime}})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{M^{\prime}})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{M^{\prime}})_{*}}

Note that by construction
\pi_{1}(\varphi_{x})=\pi_{1}(i_{M^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ(i_{M}))
and so
\varphi_{x}=i_{M^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ i_{M}\colon H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{3}(\pi_{1}(M^{\prime});\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
Since we performed the surgeries away from
\overline{\mathfrak{f}^{M}(z)}
, this immersed sphere can be viewed as an immersed sphere in
M^{\prime}
, but for clarity we denote
y:=[\overline{\mathfrak{f}(z)}]\in\pi_{2}(M^{\prime})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2
, which then satisfies
(i_{M}^{\prime})_{*}(y)=(i_{M})_{*}(\mathfrak{f}(z))
. It is not necessarily true that
y
and
\mathfrak{f}^{M^{\prime}}(\varphi_{x}(z))
agree. The relative handle chain complex for
(W,M^{\prime})
is

C_{*}(W,M^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}^{n}\quad*=3\\
0\quad\text{else}\end{cases}

for some non-negative integer
n
and hence

\ker\left((i_{M^{\prime}})_{*}\colon H_{2}(M^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z}/2)\right)\cong H_{3}(W,M^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{n},

generated by the belt
3
-discs
B_{\ell}
of the
1
-handles, and their images in
H_{2}(M^{\prime})
are the boundaries
\partial B_{\ell}
, for
{\ell}=1,\dots,n
. By an application of the snake lemma

\ker((i_{M^{\prime}})_{*}\colon\pi_{2}(M^{\prime})\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2\to\pi_{2}(W)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{n},

again generated the
\partial B_{\ell}
and so there are integers
k_{\ell}
such that

y+\sum_{\ell=1,\dots,n}k_{\ell}\partial B_{\ell}=\mathfrak{f}^{M^{\prime}}((\varphi_{x})_{*}(z)).

We obtain that

\displaystyle(\varphi_{x})_{*}d_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=d_{2}((\varphi_{x})_{*}(z))

\displaystyle=\delta_{2}^{M^{\prime},w_{2}(M^{\prime})}((\varphi_{x})_{*}(z))=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\mathfrak{f}^{M^{\prime}}((\varphi_{x})_{*}(z))+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\mathfrak{f}^{M^{\prime}}((\varphi_{x})_{*}(z)),c)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(y+\sum_{\ell=1,\dots,n}k_{\ell}\partial B_{\ell})+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(y+\sum_{\ell=1,\dots,n}k_{\ell}\partial B_{\ell},c)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(y)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(y,c)

\displaystyle=(\varphi_{x})_{*}[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{J}(\mathfrak{f}^{M}(z))+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\mathfrak{f}^{M}(z),c)]=(\varphi_{x})_{*}\delta_{2}^{M,w}(z),

where the first equality comes from naturality of the JSS; the second by
Lemma

5.9
; the third by the definition of
\delta_{2}^{M^{\prime},w_{2}(M^{\prime})}
; the fourth from the above argument; the fifth since the
\partial B_{\ell}
are all framed, embedded and disjoint from
c
; the sixth is from the definition of the element
y
; and the seventh is by the definition of
\delta_{2}^{M,w}
.

This implies that
(\varphi_{x})_{*}(\delta_{2}^{M,w}(z)-d_{2}(z))=0
, which contradicts the definition of
\varphi_{x}
. Hence
x=\delta_{2}^{M,w}(z)-d_{2}(z)=0
.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

For any closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
(M,u_{M})
with a
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism
u_{M}^{\pi}\colon M\to B\pi
, with
u^{\pi}_{M*}([M])=0
such that
\PD(w_{2}(M))
can be represented by an immersed sphere, we have an equality in
I/IJ

a\frown z={\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\mathfrak{f}^{M}(z),\PD(u_{M}^{\pi*}a))

for any
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
and any
a\in H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

Proof.

First, note that the right-hand side of the above equation is well-defined, see
Remark

3.14
.

Form a model for
B\pi
by attaching
k
-cells to
M
where
k\geq 3
. This way the map
\mathfrak{f}^{M}
has a geometric interpretation as a certain boundary map (see
Definition

A.4
). Let
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
and
a\in H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
be any elements. Represent
z
as a map from a closed 3-dimensional manifold
z\colon W^{3}\to B\pi
, which is always possible by Thom
[
28
]
. Assume that
W
has a handlebody decomposition with a single 3-handle and denote by
W^{0}
to be the manifold
W
with this handle removed. Homotope the map
z
such that
W^{0}
lands in
M
.

Choose a model for
D:=\PD((u^{\pi}_{M})^{*}(a))\in H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
given by an immersed sphere in
M
. This is possible since by assumption the lower horizontal map of the following commutative diagram is zero

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{M*}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frown u_{M}^{\pi*}[M]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{M}^{\pi*}}

and therefore
\PD((u^{\pi}_{M})^{*}(a))
is spherical. The transverse intersection of
W^{0}
and
D
is given by a
1
-dimensional manifold, which we denote by
A
, that is some collection of
\mathbb{Z}/2
-nullhomologous circles and proper arcs
\{a_{i}\}_{i=1,\dots,k}
in
W^{0}
. Close up all the arcs in
A
by arcs
\{\widetilde{a_{i}}\}_{i=1,\dots,k}
in
\partial W^{0}
(there is a unique way to do this since different choices are homologous through the top cell of
W
) to obtain a manifold
\overline{A}
that, by construction, is homologous to
a\frown z
.

By construction the element
\partial W^{0}\rightarrow M
is
\mathfrak{f}^{M}(a)\in\pi_{2}(M)\otimes\mathbb{Z}/2
. Hence the intersection
P:=\partial W^{0}\pitchfork D
, when counted with groups elements, gives the value
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\mathfrak{f}^{M}(z),a)
. Note that
\partial A=P
and using this we have a natural decomposition of
P
into sets of size two,
P=\{p_{1},q_{1}\}\cup\{p_{2},q_{2}\}\cup\dots\{p_{k},q_{k}\}
, where each pair
\{p_{i},q_{i}\}
is connected by two arcs
a_{i}\subseteq A,\widetilde{a_{i}}\subseteq\overline{A}\setminus\overline{{\overline{A}\setminus A}}
. What is left to show is that the
1
-dimensional manifold
\overline{A}
is homologous to the double point loops of
\partial W^{0}
and
D
.

Fix a basepoint
x_{0}\in M
. For every pair
\{p_{i},q_{i}\}\subset P
we fix paths
\gamma_{i}
,
\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}
from
x_{0}
to
p_{i}
in
D
and
\partial W^{0}
, respectively. For
p\in P
let
\theta(p)
be the
\pi_{1}
element associated to the point
p
. Then

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(\mathfrak{f}(z),a)=\sum_{p\in P}\theta(p)=\sum_{i=1,\dots,k}\theta(p_{i})+\theta(q_{i})=\sum_{i=1,\dots,k}\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}\gamma_{i}+\widetilde{\gamma_{i}}\widetilde{a_{i}}a_{i}\gamma_{i}=\sum_{i=1,\dots,k}\widetilde{a_{i}}a_{i}=\overline{A},

where the first equality is by the definition of
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}
, the second is by reindexing, the third is by the observation above, the fourth holds since we are working in homology, and the last holds by the decomposition of
\overline{A}
into arcs
\widetilde{a}_{i}a_{i}
and null-homologous circles.
∎

We can now finally prove
Proposition

5.7
.

Proof of
Proposition

5.7
.

We need to show that

\delta_{2}^{X,w}(z)=d_{2}(z)

for all
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. By
Proposition

B.9
,
d_{2}
is dual to

d^{2}(-)=-\frown w_{2}^{\pi}+Sq^{1}(-)\frown w_{1}^{\pi}.

By
Proposition

5.6
there exists a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
M
such that
\delta_{2}^{X,w}=\delta_{2}^{M,w_{M}}
,
(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}([M])=0
and the Poincaré dual of the obstruction class
w_{M}=\PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi}))
spherical. Since the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\PD}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\frown(u_{M}^{\pi}[M]})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}}

commutes and
(u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}([M])=0
, we have that
\PD((u^{\pi}_{M})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi}))
is spherical. By decomposing
w_{M}
as
w_{M}=\PD(w_{2}(M)+(u^{\pi}_{M})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi}))
we see that
\PD(w_{2}(M))
is also spherical. Furthermore, by
Lemma

2.13
we have that both
\PD(w_{2}(M))
and
\PD((u^{\pi}_{M})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi}))
are
s
-characteristic. These facts give us the following description of
\delta_{2}^{M,w_{M}}
.

\displaystyle\delta_{2}^{M,w_{M}}(z)

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(\mathfrak{f}(z))+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\mathfrak{f}(z),\PD(w_{2}(M)+(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi})))

\displaystyle=\delta_{2}^{M,\PD(w_{2}(M))}(z)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\mathfrak{f}(z),(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi}))

\displaystyle=(w_{1}^{\pi}\smile\Sq^{1}(-))^{*}(z)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(\mathfrak{f}(z),(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi}))

\displaystyle=(w_{1}^{\pi}\smile\Sq^{1}(-))^{*}(z)+(u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi})\frown z

\displaystyle=(w_{1}^{\pi}\smile\Sq^{1}(-))^{*}(z)+((u_{M}^{\pi})^{*}(w_{2}^{\pi})\smile-)^{*}(z),

where the first equality is by the definition of
\delta_{2}^{M,w_{M}}
, the second is by the above and the definition of
\delta_{2}^{M,\PD(w_{2}(M))}
, the third is by
Proposition

5.8
, the fourth is by
Lemma

5.12
, and the last is by the cup-cap formula.
∎

Everything we have done so far implies
B
. For the reader’s convenience, we spell this out now.

Proof of
B
.

Let
x\in H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})
. By an analogous argument to that in
Section

4.1
there exists a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
M
such that
u_{M}^{\pi}[M]=x
. Then immediately
Lemma

5.2
implies that
\delta_{2}^{4,0}(x)=d_{2}^{4,0}(x)
.

Let
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Pick
(Y,u_{Y})
a
\xi
-3-manifold such that
\secondary(Y,u_{Y})=0
. By
5.4
there exists a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
with obstruction class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
Poincaré-Lefschetz dual to some
w\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
with a spherical representative. By
Proposition

5.7
we have that
\delta_{2}^{X,w}(z)=d_{2}^{(3,1)}(z)
. This implies that we can define
\delta_{2}^{(3,1)}:=\delta_{2}^{X,w}
since it is independent of
X
and
w
; tautologically this implies that
\delta_{2}^{(3,1)}=d_{2}^{(3,1)}
.
∎

Similarly, this implies
C
.

Proof of
C
.

Let
\pi:=\pi_{1}(X)
and let
\xi=\xi(\pi,0,0)
. The spin structure
s
gives us a
\xi
-structure on
Y
. From
Proposition

2.8
one can compute that
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})=w_{2}^{s}
, the relative Stiefel-Whitney class. Let
b
be a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-null-homologous immersed sphere in
X
. By the Serre exact sequence we have that there exists
z\in H_{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
such that
b
is a lift of
\mathfrak{f}(z)
. For the normal 1-type
\xi
we have that

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(b,c)-\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(b)=\delta_{2}^{X,w}(z)=\delta_{2}^{(3,1)}(z)=d_{2}^{(3,1)}(z)=0

where all equalities are modulo
IJ
. The first follows from
Proposition

5.5
; the second and the third follow from
B
; and the fourth follows from
Proposition

B.9
, since
w_{2}^{\pi}
and
w_{1}^{\pi}
are both zero. The result is now immediate.
∎

5.3.
The geometric differential
\delta_{3}^{(4,0)}

In this subsection fix
\xi=\xi(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
a normal
1
-type. We will now define the third geometric differential

\delta_{3}^{(4,0)}\colon\ker(\delta^{(4,0)}_{2}\colon H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})\to H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2))\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(\delta_{2}).

Definition 5.13
.

Represent
x\in\ker(\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}\colon H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})\to H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2))
by a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
, with a
c_{M}
a spherical representative for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
with respect to
\xi
. Then we define

\delta_{3}^{(4,0)}(x):=\terG(M,u_{M}^{\pi},c_{M})=[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c_{M})]\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(\delta_{2}^{(3,1)}).

Note that the above definition is analogous to the definition of
\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}
(see
Definition

5.1
) in that both are defined as the secondary/tertiary invariant of some closed
4
-manifold.

Similarly to
\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}
we have to prove that this is well-defined, i.e. does not depend on the choice of a manifold representing
x
. For
\delta_{2}^{(4,0)}
we did this by showing that it was equal to the JSS
d_{2}
. We will not do that here.

Proposition 5.14
.

The above definition of
\delta_{3}^{(4,0)}
is well-defined, i.e. it defines a map

\delta_{3}^{(4,0)}:\ker(\delta^{(4,0)}_{2}\colon H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})\to H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2))\to H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(\delta_{2}^{(3,1)}).

Proof.

First we show that
\delta_{3}
does not depend on the choice of
c_{M}
. Let
c_{M},c^{\prime}_{M}\in\pi_{2}(X)
be elements with
h_{2}(c_{M})=h_{2}(c^{\prime}_{M})=\PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}))
and set
a=c_{M}-c_{M}^{\prime}\in\ker h_{2}
. Now we obtain

\displaystyle\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c_{M})-\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c^{\prime}_{M})

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c^{\prime}_{M}+a)-\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c^{\prime}_{M})

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c^{\prime}_{M})+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(a,c_{M})-\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c^{\prime}_{M})

\displaystyle=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(a)+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(c^{\prime}_{M},a)=\delta^{(3,1)}_{2}(a),

where the second equality follows from
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}
being the
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinear form corresponding to
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}
(
Proposition

3.9
) and the rest are immediate.

Secondly we need to show that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c_{M})\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(\delta_{2})
only depends on
u_{M*}^{\pi}[M]
. Two closed
4
-dimensional manifolds
M_{1},M_{2}
with identified fundamental groups
\pi
are
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
-stable diffeomorphic
4
4

4

i.e. diffeomorphic after connected-summing with copies of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
and
\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
on both sides.
if and only if their fundamental classes agree in
H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}^{\pi}})
. Thus, it suffices to show that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c_{M})
only depends on the
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
-stable class of
M
, i.e. that if
N=\mathbb{CP}^{2}
or
N=\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
and we take any class
c_{M\#N}\in\pi_{2}(M\#N)
such that
h_{2}(c_{M\#N})=\PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M\#N,u_{M\#N}^{\pi}))
we get
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c_{M\#N})=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)
in
H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(\delta_{2})
. Recall that by
Proposition

5.5
the choice of
c_{M\#N}
does not matter, as long as it represents the characteristic element. We only carry out the proof for
N=\mathbb{CP}^{2}
; the other case is analogous. Choose a representative for
c_{M\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
to be a connected sum of a class
c\in\pi_{2}(M)
and the class
[\mathbb{CP}^{1}]\in\pi_{2}(M\#\mathbb{CP}^{2})
. Now

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c+[\mathbb{CP}^{1}])=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}([\mathbb{CP}^{1}])+{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}_{J}(c,[\mathbb{CP}^{1}])=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2}),

where the first equality follows from
Proposition

3.9
, and the second since
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
is embedded and disjoint from
c
.
∎

5.4.
Well-definedness of the geometric tertiary invariant

We now take the definition of the geometric tertiary obstruction given in
Section

4
and show that we can make it only depend on the bounding
\xi
-3-manifold once we quotient out by the differentials
\delta_{2}^{(3,1)}
and
\delta_{3}^{(4,0)}
. This is encapsulated by the following theorem.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-3-manifold with two
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-fillings
(X,u^{\pi}_{X})
and
(X^{\prime},u^{\pi}_{X^{\prime}})
such that the obstruction
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u^{\pi}_{X})
has two spherical representatives
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
, and such that
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X^{\prime},Y,u_{Y},u^{\pi}_{X^{\prime}})
has some spherical representative
c^{\prime}
. Then we have the following.

(1)

\terG(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi},c_{1})-\terG(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi},c_{2})\in\im(\delta_{2}),

(2)
