\terG(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi},c_{1})-\terG(X^{\prime},Y,u_{Y},u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi},c^{\prime})\in\im(\delta_{2},\delta_{3}).

A direct consequence of
Theorem

5.15
is that the following are well-defined.

(a)

\terG(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi}):=[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)]\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(\delta_{2})
for some choice of
c
representing
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u^{\pi}_{X})
.

(b)

\terG(Y,u_{Y}):=[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)]\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/(\im(\delta_{2}),\im(\delta_{3}))
for some choice of
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
and
c
representing
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u^{\pi}_{X})
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.15
.

Note that (
1
) was already proved in the proof of
Proposition

5.14
.

We now prove (
2
). Let
w\in H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
and
w^{\prime}\in H_{2}(X^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}/2)
be the Poincaré-Leftschetz duals of
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
and
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X^{\prime},Y,u_{Y},u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi})
, respectively. Let

(M,u_{M}^{\pi}):=(X\cup_{Y}\overline{X^{\prime}},u_{X}^{\pi}\cup_{u_{Y}}u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi})

be the closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold obtained by gluing together
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
and
(X^{\prime},u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi})
together along
(Y,u_{Y})
. By
Lemma

2.11
the obstruction to
X\cup X_{2}
having a
\xi
-structure is the sum
w+w^{\prime}\in H_{2}(X_{1}\cup\overline{X_{2}};\mathbb{Z}/2)
, represented by
c:=c_{1}\#c^{\prime}
. Surger the map
u_{M}^{\pi}
away from
c
to be an isomorphism on fundamental groups, such that
c
still represents the obstruction to
M
admitting a
\xi
-structure. Using
Proposition

5.14
we see that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(w+w^{\prime})\in\im(\delta_{3})
.
∎

6.
Vanishing of the geometric tertiary obstruction

This section is first concerned with proving
A
. The main result in that direction is the following.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal 1-type and let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-3-manifold with
\pri(Y,u_{Y})=0=\secondary(Y,u_{Y})
. Then
\terG(Y,u_{Y})=0
if and only if there exists a
\xi
-filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
.

Recall that we have already seen that the vanishing of primary obstruction is equivalent to the existence of a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling (
4.2
). We have also seen that the vanishing of the secondary obstruction is equivalent to the existence of a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling
X
such that the obstruction class to extending the
\xi
-structure is spherical (
5.4
). We will take the consequences of these two lemmas to be our starting point for proving
Theorem

6.1
, i.e. we will refer to the following setup.

Setup 6.2
.

We have a
\xi
-3-manifold
(Y,u_{Y})
and a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling
(X,u^{\pi}_{X})
such that the Poincaré dual of the obstruction
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u^{\pi}_{X},u_{Y})\in H^{2}(X,Y;\mathbb{Z}/2)
has a spherical representative
c\in\pi_{2}(X)
.

We now begin a sequence of lemmas which concern the various consequences if
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)
‘vanishes’ to different extents.

Lemma 6.3
.

Assume we are in the situation described by
6.2
. If
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(c)=0\in I\subset\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
then either

(a)

The class
c
can be represented by an embedding in
X\#k(S^{2}\times S^{2})
for some
k
.

(b)

There exists an immersion
c^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}
such that
[c^{\prime}]\in H_{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is characteristic and such that
c\#c^{\prime}
is represented by an embedding in
X\#2\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#k(S^{2}\times S^{2})
for some
k
.

In either case, we can find a new null-bordism of
(Y,u_{Y})
such that the obstruction class to it being a null-
\xi
-bordism can be embedded.

Proof.

Here we make use of
Section

3.4
and the Kervaire-Milnor invariant. The rough idea of the argument is as follows. First, argue that we can arrange that
\km(c)=0
, and then apply
3.19
to make
c
embedded. While doing this we check that
c
remains a representative for our obstruction class throughout our modifications.

We begin by assuming that
\km(c)=1
, since otherwise we may skip the step of arranging that
\km(c)=0
. Now we freely arrange that
c
has an algebraic dual sphere by connected-summing with
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
and tubing
c
into
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}
(note that since
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is simply-connected and oriented, there is no issue in extending the
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-structure). Since the
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}
is a representative of
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(\mathbb{CP}^{2},\mathbb{CP}^{2}\xrightarrow{*}B\pi)
, it is clear that
c\#\mathbb{CP}^{1}
is a representative for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2},Y,u_{X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}}^{\pi},u_{Y})
. We subsume the
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
into
X
and assume that our
c
has an algebraic dual sphere.

Again form the connected-sum
X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}
and let
b
be the immersed sphere in
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
given by
3.20
which is characteristic, has
\km(b)=1
and
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(b)=0
. Tube
c
into
b
to form
c\#b
, noting that
c\#b
is again a representative for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2},Y,u_{X\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}}^{\pi},u_{Y})
, since
b
is characteristic. We split into two cases. First assume that
c\#b
is r-characteristic. Then, by
Theorem

3.18
and that
c
has an algebraic dual sphere, we can calculate that
\km(c\#b)=1+1=0
, since a collection of Whitney discs for the double-points of
c\#b
is given by a union of the Whitney discs for
b
and
c
separately, and these Whitney discs are contained inside their respective connected-summands. In the second case we assume that
c\#b
is not r-characteristic. Then by
Theorem

3.18
and that
c
has an algebraic dual sphere,
\km(c\#b)=0
. This completes the argument that
\km(c\#b)=0
. Again we subsume the
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
into
X
and assume that our
c
has
\km(c)=0
.

We now use
3.19
and stably embed
c
. One should note that, since
S^{2}\times S^{2}
is a simply-connected spin manifold, there is no issue in extending the
\xi
-structure when taking connected-sums with
S^{2}\times S^{2}
, and hence
c
is still a representative for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2}),Y,u_{Y},u^{\pi}_{X\#(S^{2}\times S^{2})}
).
∎

The following three lemmas are a consequence of the proof of
Theorem

5.15

Lemma 6.4
.

Assume we are in the situation described by
6.2
. If
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)=0\in I/IJ
then there exists an additional closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
X^{\prime}
and an immersion
c^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
such that
c\#c^{\prime}\subset X\#X^{\prime}
represents the obstruction to extending the
\xi
-structure across
X\#X^{\prime}
and
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(c\#c^{\prime})=0\in I\subset\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.7
the kernel of the map
f\colon I\to I/IJ
is generated by
\Lambda
-linear combinations of
\{2g\mid g\in\pi\}
and
\{gh-g-h+1\mid g,h\in\pi\}
. By assumption, we have that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(c)\in\ker(f)
. For each of these generators
\omega\in\ker(f)
we will exhibit a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
and an element
c_{M}\in\pi_{2}(M)
such that under the Hurewicz map
h_{2}(c_{M})=\PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi}))
.

Pick an element
g\in\pi
other than the identity. Consider
M=S^{1}\times S^{3}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}
in the case that
w_{1}^{\pi}(g)
is trivial and
M=S^{1}\widetilde{\times}S^{3}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}
in the case that
w_{1}^{\pi}(g)
is nontrivial. Denote the generator of the fundamental group of
M
by
t
. There is a map
M\to B\pi
, which sends
t
to
g
and we use this to endow
M
with a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-structure. Let
p=(2-t)[\mathbb{CP}^{1}]\in\pi_{2}(M)
. This element has the property that
h_{2}(p)=\PD(w_{2}(M))=\PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
. By
Lemma

3.4
we have

\begin{cases}w_{1}^{\pi}(t)\text{ trivial}&{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(p,p)=(2-t)\overline{(2-t)}-1=4-2t-2t^{-1}=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(p)+\overline{\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(p)}\\
w_{1}^{\pi}(t)\text{ non-trivial}&{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(p,p)=(2-t)\overline{(2-t)}-3=-2t+2t^{-1}=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(p)+\overline{\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(p)}\end{cases}

and from this we conclude that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}((2-t)[\mathbb{CP}^{1}])=(2-2t)
. From the connected-sum
X\#M
form
X^{\prime}
by representing the loop
gt^{-1}
away from the representatives of
c
and
p
and surgering that loop in a
\xi
-preserving way. Note that in particular the loop
gt^{-1}
is framable. It can be seen that the new obstruction class
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X^{\prime},Y,u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi},u_{Y})
is represented by the immersion
c\#p
. Since the surgery was performed away from
c
or
p
we obtain that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(w\#p)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(w)+2-2g
.

Pick
g,h\in\pi
two nonzero elements. Consider

M=\begin{cases}S^{1}\!\times S^{3}\#S^{1}\!\times S^{3}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}\quad\text{ if }w_{1}^{\pi}(g)=0,w_{1}^{\pi}(h)=0\\
S^{1}\widetilde{\times}S^{3}\#S^{1}\!\times S^{3}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}\quad\text{ if }w_{1}^{\pi}(g)=1,w_{1}^{\pi}(h)=0\\
S^{1}\!\times S^{3}\#S^{1}\widetilde{\times}S^{3}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}\quad\text{ if }w_{1}^{\pi}(g)=0,w_{1}^{\pi}(h)=1\\
S^{1}\widetilde{\times}S^{3}\#S^{1}\widetilde{\times}S^{3}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}\quad\text{ if }w_{1}^{\pi}(g)=1,w_{1}^{\pi}(h)=1\\
\end{cases}

and fix the two generators
t,s
of
\pi_{1}(M)
. There is a map
M\to B\pi
which sends
t
to
g
and
s
to
h
, and using this map endows
M
with a
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
structure. Pick
p=(t+s^{-1}-1)[\mathbb{CP}^{1}]
. By
Lemma

3.4
and for example for
w^{\pi}_{1}(t)=0=w_{1}^{\pi}(s)
we see that

{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}(p,p)=(t+s^{-1}-1)\overline{(t+s^{-1}-1)}=ts+st-s-s^{-1}-t-t^{-1}+3,

and so
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(p)=ts-s-t
. It can be verified that for all other combinations of
w_{1}^{\pi}(t)
and
w_{1}^{\pi}(t)
the result is the same. By the same argument as before, form
X\#M
and perform two
\xi
-preserving surgeries on
gt^{-1}
and
hs^{-1}
and observe
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(w\#p)=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(w)+gh-g-h
.

Now performing some finite combination of the above operations completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.5
.

Assume we are in the situation described by
6.2
. If
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)\in\im\delta_{2}^{(3,1)}
then there exists a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-null-homologous
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)
such that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c\#\alpha)=0\in I/IJ\cong H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.

Proof.

Let
z\in H_{3}(X;\mathbb{Z}/2)
be such that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{J}(c)=\delta_{2}^{(3,1)}(z)
and choose any lift
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(X)
of
\mathfrak{f}^{X}(z)
. Then since
\alpha
is
\mathbb{Z}/2
-nullhomologous we can calculate like in the proof of
Proposition

5.14
that

\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c\#\alpha)-\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)=\delta^{(3,1)}_{2}(z),

showing that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c\#\alpha)=0
in
I/IJ
.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Assume we are in the situation described by
6.2
. If
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)\in\im\delta_{3}
then there exists a different manifold
X^{\prime}
with
c^{\prime}\in\pi_{2}(X^{\prime})
like in the
6.2
such that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c^{\prime})=0\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im\delta_{2}
.

Proof.

Since
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)\in\im\delta^{(4,0)}_{3}
there exists an element
a\in H_{4}(\pi;Z^{w_{1}^{\pi}})
which hits it. Therefore there exists a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
M^{4}
with
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
represented by
c_{M}\pi_{2}(M)
such that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c_{M})=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)
. Let
(X\#M)^{\prime}
denote the result of surgering the map
u_{X}^{\pi}\#u_{M}^{\pi}
to a
\pi_{1}
-equivalence, where the surgeries are performed away from some fixed representatives of
c
and
c_{M}
in a
\xi
-preserving way. Then

[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c\#c_{M})]=[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)+\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c_{M})]=0\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/(\im\delta_{2}^{(3,1)})

where the first equality comes from the fact that
c
and
c_{M}
are disjoint in
(X\#M)^{\prime}
along with
Proposition

3.9
, and the second equality is by our assumption.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

6.1
.

We start with the ‘if’ direction, i.e. we assume that there exists a
\xi
-filling
(X,u_{X})
of
(Y,u_{Y})
. We can choose a representative for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u^{\pi}_{X})
to be a local unknot which bounds an embedded
D^{3}
and hence
\terG(Y,u_{Y})=0
.

We now consider the ‘only if’ direction. By
4.2
and
5.4
we can assume that we are in situation described in
6.2
. We now prove that we can find a new
\xi
-filling for
(Y,u_{Y})
such that the
c
is embedded.

By
Lemma

6.6
and
Lemma

6.5
we can assume that our filling
X
has a representative
c
for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
with
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)=0\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. By
Lemma

6.4
we can further assume that
c
has the property that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(c)=0\in\mathbb{Z}[\pi]
and by
Lemma

6.3
this implies that we can assume that
c
is embedded. All the steps above allow us to change our filling manifold
X
.

Now, generalising an argument due to Kervaire-Milnor
[
14
]
, we can arrange that
c
has Euler number
+1
by connected-summing with
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
and
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
and tubing into the
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{2}
or
\mathbb{CP}^{1}\subset\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
if necessary. Blowing down
c
, i.e. replacing the tubular neighbourhood of
c
by a copy of
D^{4}
, reduces the problem to extending the given
\xi
-structure on
S^{3}
to
D^{4}
, which we now briefly demonstrate can always be achieved.

Consider the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BO}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)\times K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}^{\pi}\times w_{2}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{S^{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{D^{4}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}\times w_{2}}

By the definition of the normal 1-type
B
, we have that
u_{S^{3}}
extends to
D^{4}
provided that we can extend the map
p\circ u_{S}^{3}
such that the corresponding relative cohomology class in
H^{1}(D^{4},S^{3};\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus H^{2}(D^{4},S^{3};\mathbb{Z}/2)
given by postcomposing with
w_{1}^{\pi}\times w_{2}^{\pi}
is trivial, but this is clear since the cohomology groups are themselves trivial. This means that we have shown that
(Y,u_{Y})
is
\xi
-nullbordant, which finishes the proof.
∎

As a consequence of
Theorem

6.1
we have the following result relating the tertiary and geometric tertiary invariants.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-3-manifold, with
\pri(Y,u_{Y})=0
and
\secondary(Y,u_{Y})=0
. Then the geometric tertiary invariant
\terG(Y,u_{Y})
vanishes if and only if the usual tertiary invariant
\ter(Y,u_{Y})
vanishes.

Proof.

Assume that
\terG(Y,u_{Y})
vanishes. Then by
Theorem

6.1
there exists a
\xi
-filling and hence
\ter(Y,u_{Y})
must also vanish. Similarly if
\ter(Y,u_{Y})
vanishes then the standard AHSS/JSS argument proves that there exists a
\xi
-filling and hence by
Theorem

6.1
we must have that
\terG(Y,u_{Y})
vanishes.
∎

We can now prove
A
from the introduction.

Proof of
A
.

We have that
u_{Y}^{\pi}([Y])=0
if and only if
(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{2,1}
by
4.1
. Furthermore,
(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{2,1}
if and only if there exists a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
by
4.2
. We now assume this filling
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
exists. We have that
(u_{X}^{\pi})_{*}\PD(\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi}))=0
if and only if
(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{1,2}
by
5.3
. Furthermore
(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{1,2}
if and only if there exits a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling such that
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
has a spherical representative by
5.4
. We now assume such a filling
(X^{\prime},u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi})
exists with
c
a spherical representative for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X^{\prime}}^{\pi})
. We have that
\terG(Y,u_{Y})=[\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)]=0
if and only if there exists a
\xi
-filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
by
Theorem

6.1
. Note that this is equivalent to
(Y,u_{Y})\in F_{0,3}
since
F_{0,3}=\{0\}\in\Omega_{3}^{\xi}
.
∎

6.1.
Comparing
\terG
and
\ter
.

We now show that
\ter
and
\terG
are ‘equal’. We will make what this means precise later, but for now we need a realisation result for
\terG
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
be a normal
1
-type and let
g\in\pi
. Then there is a compact
4
-dimensional manifold
V_{g}
and a map
u_{V_{g}}^{\pi}\colon V_{g}\to B\pi
such that

(1)

\partial V_{g}=S^{1}\times S^{1}\times S^{1}
if
w_{1}^{\pi}(g)=0
,
\partial V_{g}=S^{1}\times K
, for
K
a Klein bottle if
w_{1}^{\pi}(g)\neq 0
.

(2)

There exists an identification
\pi_{1}(V_{g})\cong\mathbb{Z}
such that under this identification
u_{V_{g}}^{\pi}
sends
+1
to
g
.

(3)

u_{V_{g}}^{\pi*}(w_{1}^{\pi})=w_{1}(V_{g})
.

(4)

The (normal) second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}(V_{g})
vanishes and so the normal
1
-type of
V_{g}
is
\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}(V_{g}),0)
.

(5)

There exists a
\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}(V_{g}),0)
-structure
p
on
\partial V_{g}
, inducing the map
\pi_{1}(\partial V_{g})\to\pi_{1}(V_{g})\cong\mathbb{Z}
such that the obstruction
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(V_{g},\partial V_{g},p,u_{V_{g}}^{\pi})
is the Poincaré dual of an immersed sphere
S
in
V_{g}
such that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(S)=t-1\in I/IJ
.

Proof.

Figure 3.
On the left: a Kirby diagram for the manifold
V_{g}
if
g
is orientable. On the right: a Kirby diagram for the manifold
V_{g}
if
g
is non-orientable. The notation for the 1-handles is due to Akbulut
[
1
, Sections 1.1, 1.5]

Let

Y_{g}=\begin{cases}S^{1}\times S^{1}\times S^{1}&w_{1}(g)=0\\
S^{1}\times K&w_{1}(g)\neq 0,\end{cases}

where
K:=S^{1}\widetilde{\times}S^{1}
denotes the Klein bottle. In the non-orientable case the orientation character is given by the composition
S^{1}\times(S^{1}\widetilde{\times}S^{1})\xrightarrow{q}S^{1}\to B\mathbb{Z}/2
(here
q
is the projection onto
K
postcomposed with the fibre bundle map). In both cases above
V_{g}
will be defined as a suitable filling of
Y
.

If
w_{1}(g)=0
, define
u_{Y_{g}}^{\pi}\colon Y_{g}=S^{1}\times S^{1}\times S^{1}\xrightarrow{\pr_{3}}S^{1}=B\mathbb{Z}
, where
\pr_{3}
denotes projection onto the third coordinate. We describe a
(B\mathbb{Z},0)
-filling of
Y_{g}
by taking the filling
S^{1}\times D^{2}\times S^{1}
and surgering out the kernel of
\pi_{1}(u_{Y_{g}}^{\pi})
to form the manifold
V_{g}
, which is described using a Kirby diagram in
Figure

3
. Fix the induced
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism
V_{g}\to B\mathbb{Z}
which restricts to
u_{Y_{g}}^{\pi}
on the boundary.

If
w_{1}(g)\neq 0
, define
u_{Y_{g}}^{\pi}\colon Y_{g}=S^{1}\times S^{1}\widetilde{\times}S^{1}\xrightarrow{q}B\mathbb{Z}=S^{1}
. Note that the first Stiefel-Whitney class of
Y_{g}
factors through
u_{Y_{g}}^{\pi}
. We describe a
(B\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling of
Y_{g}
by taking the filling
S^{1}\times H
where
H
denotes the solid Klein bottle, and then surgering out the kernel of
\pi_{1}(u_{Y_{g}}^{\pi})
to form
V_{g}
, which is described using a Kirby diagram in
Figure

3
. Fix the induced
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism
V_{g}\to B\mathbb{Z}
which restricts to
u_{Y_{g}}^{\pi}
on the boundary.

Now (
1
), (
2
) and (
3
) are immediate. The integral intersection form on the second homology of manifolds in
Figure

3
is even in both cases, so the second
tangential
Wu class, equal to the second
normal
Stiefel-Whitney vanishes, establishing (
4
) (this follows since there is no
2
-torsion in the fundamental group of
V_{g}
).

Pick
p_{B}
to be some
\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{\pi},0)
-structure on
V_{g}
. Recall from
Remark

2.5
that a
\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{\pi},0)
structure on a manifold
Y_{g}
is a map
u_{Y_{g}}^{\pi}\colon Y_{g}\to B\mathbb{Z}
over
w_{1}^{\pi}
and a
\Pin^{+}
-structure on
Y_{g}
. Since we already have the former, we now define a suitable
\Pin^{+}
-structure. The handle decomposition given in
Figure

3
defines an isomorphism

H^{2}(V_{g},\partial V_{g};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong H_{2}(V_{g};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to(\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/2).

Let
x\in H^{1}(Y_{g};\mathbb{Z}/2)
be any preimage of
(1,1)\in H^{2}(V_{g},Y_{g};\mathbb{Z}/2)
(note that this exists since the map
H^{1}(Y_{g};\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H^{2}(V_{g},Y_{g};\mathbb{Z}/2)
is surjective).
\Pin^{+}
-structures on
Y_{g}
are a torsor over
H^{1}(Y_{g};\mathbb{Z}/2)
and so we can define
p=p_{B}|_{Y_{g}}\cdot x
.

The
\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{\pi},0)
-structure on
Y_{g}
does not extend to
V_{g}
because
x
does not come from
H^{1}(V_{g};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. By construction, the obstruction
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(V_{g},\partial V_{g},p,V_{g}\to B\mathbb{Z})
to
p
extending to
V_{g}
is represented in either case by an immersed 2-sphere
S
consisting of the core of both handles, connected by the obvious annulus in
Figure

3
.
Both of these spheres have two self intersections, only one of them with non-trivial group element giving
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{J}(S)=t-1
. This proves (
5
).
∎

To show that
\ter
and
\terG
agree, we must show they land in the same group. In
Proposition

5.7
we have already shown that
d_{2},\delta_{2}
coincide, in particular we have to show that
\im(d_{3})=\im(\delta_{3})\subseteq H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2})
. We show the two inclusions separately.

Lemma 6.9
.

For
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
a normal
1
-type we have for
d_{3}:E^{3}_{4,0}\to E^{3}_{1,2}
the differential of the JSS and
\delta_{3}
the geometric map defined in
Definition

5.13
that

\im(\delta_{3})\subseteq\im(d_{3})\subset H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2}).

Proof.

Let
x\in\im\delta_{3}\subset H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2})
be a nonzero element. Then we can represent the equivalence class
[x]\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2},d_{3})
by a
\xi
-3-manifold
(Y,u_{Y})
that lies in the corresponding bordism class.
Corollary

6.7
implies that
(Y,u_{Y})
is
\xi
-null-bordant and hence
x\in\im d_{3}
.
∎

Theorem 6.10
.

Fix
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
a normal
1
-type. Let
(Y,u_{Y})
be a
\xi
-3-manifold, with
\pri(Y,u_{Y})=0=\secondary(Y,u_{Y})
.
Then

[\terG(Y,u_{Y})]=\ter(Y,u_{Y})\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2},d_{3}).

Proof.

If
\terG(Y,u_{Y})=0
we have already seen in
Corollary

6.7
that
\ter(Y,u_{Y})=0
. So we assume
\terG(Y,u_{Y})
is nonzero.

Let
(X,u_{X}^{\pi})
be a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling of
(Y,u_{Y})
, and further let
c
be a spherical representative for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(X,Y,u_{Y},u_{X}^{\pi})
(see
5.4
). Let
g\in\pi
be any lift of
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
. By assumption
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)
is nontrivial and so
g
is non-trivial. We have that
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)=g-1\in I/IJ
(see
Lemma

3.6
(
4
) for the isomorphism
H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong I/IJ
). By abuse of notation, we choose a map
g\colon B\mathbb{Z}\to B\pi
that sends the generator to
g
. Fix a homotopy
H\colon B\mathbb{Z}\times I\to B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
between the following maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}^{\pi}\times w_{2}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}^{g}\times*}

and use it to obtain a map
\varphi_{H}\colon B_{\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{g},0)}\to B_{\xi}
over
BO

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{g},0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\xi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BO}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{Z}/2\times B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}^{g}\times*}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}^{\pi}\times w_{2}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{1}\times w_{2}}

which maps
\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{\pi},0)
-manifolds to
\xi
-manifolds. Furthermore, this gives a map of Thom spectra
M\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{\pi},0)\to M\xi
which induces a map of bordism groups

(\varphi_{H})_{*}\colon\Omega_{3}^{\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{g},0)}\rightarrow\Omega_{3}^{\xi}.

Let
V
be the manifold given by
Proposition

6.8
for the element
g
and the orientation character
w_{1}^{g}
. We will use the notation from that proposition throughout this proof.

By (
4
) of
Proposition

6.8

V
admits a 1-smoothing
p_{B}
with respect to
\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}(V),0)
which we now fix. By (
5
) of
Proposition

6.8
there is a
\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}(V),0)
-structure
p
on
\partial V
whose obstruction to extending to
V
is some immersed sphere
S
with
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}(S)=t-1
, where
t
is the generator of
\pi_{1}(V)
. There is an action of
H^{1}(\partial V;\mathbb{Z}/2)
on the set of
\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}(V),0)
-structures on
\partial V
, given by the
H
-space structure on
B^{2}\mathbb{Z}/2
. Denote by
x\in H^{1}(\partial V;\mathbb{Z}/2)
the difference of these structures, i.e.
x:=p-p_{B}|_{\partial V}
. It holds that under the connecting homomorphism

H^{1}(\partial V;\mathbb{Z}/2)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{d}}H^{2}(V,\partial V;\mathbb{Z}/2)

we have
\mathfrak{d}(x)=\PD^{-1}(S)
.

Define
[\partial V,u_{B}]=\varphi_{H*}([\partial V,p_{B}|_{\partial V}])
, which gives a
\xi
-structure on
\partial V
extending to
V
. Define
[\partial V,u]:=[\partial V,u_{B}]\cdot x
. Equivalently we have
\varphi_{H}([\partial V,p])=[\partial V,u]
. From
\mathfrak{d}(x)=\PD^{-1}(S)
we see that
S
is indeed also the obstruction to extending
u
to
V
. For a proof use an argument analogous to
[
15
, Page 521]
.

We now show that
\terG(Y,u_{Y})=\ter(Y,u_{Y})
. By assumption we have that
\terG((Y,u_{Y})+(\partial V,u))=0
and by
Corollary

6.7
we have
\ter((Y,u_{Y})+(\partial V,u))=0
. Now
\terG(Y,u_{Y})=\ter(Y,u_{Y})
if and only if
\terG(\partial V,u)=\ter(\partial V,u)
.

Using the James spectral sequence we find that
\ter^{\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{g},0)}\in H_{1}(B\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{Z}/2)\cong I/IJ\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, generated by
t-1
, is the only obstruction to an element being trivial in the bordism group
\Omega_{3}^{\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{g},0)}
. But
\terG(\partial V,p)=t-1\in H_{1}(B\mathbb{Z};\mathbb{Z}/2)
. By
Corollary

6.7

\ter(\partial V,p)
cannot vanish and so
\ter(\partial V,p)=t-1
. Now since
(\varphi_{H})_{*}
comes from a map of Thom spectra we have the following naturality of the tertiary obstructions

g_{*}(\ter^{\xi(\mathbb{Z},w_{1}^{g},0)}([\partial V,p]))=\ter^{\xi}(\varphi_{H}([\partial V,p]))=\ter^{\xi}(\partial V,u)

and so
\ter^{\xi}(\partial V,u)=g-1
. Since
V
is a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{g})
-filling of
\partial V
we use
Lemma

2.7
to surger
V
to make the map
V\to B\pi
a
\pi_{1}
-isomorphism. These surgeries can be made away from a representative of some spherical representative of
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(V,\partial V,p,g)
and so we can calculate
\terG(V,u)
as follows

\terG(V,u)=g_{*}(\terG(V,p))=g_{*}(t-1)=g-1.

As explained above the result now follows.
∎

Proposition 6.11
.

For
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
a normal
1
-type we have for
d_{3}:E^{3}_{4,0}\to E^{3}_{1,2}
the differential of the JSS and
\delta_{3}
the geometric map defined in
Definition

5.13
that

\im(d_{3})=\im(\delta_{3})\subset H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2}).

Proof.

In
Lemma

6.9
we have already shown that
\im(\delta_{3})\subseteq\im(d_{3})
.

Assume that
x\in\im d_{3}\subseteq H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2})
. Represent
x
by an element of
\pi
. By
Proposition

6.8
there exists a
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-filling
(V,u_{V}^{\pi})
of a
\xi
-3-manifold
(\partial V,u_{\partial V})
with a spherical representative
c
for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(V,\partial V,u_{\partial V},u_{V}^{\pi})
having
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)=x-1\in I/IJ
and so
\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)=x\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2})
. Now by
Theorem

6.10
and our assumption we have that

0=[x]=[\terG(V,\partial V,u_{\partial V},u_{V}^{\pi})]=\ter(\partial V,u_{\partial V})\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2},d_{3}).

By the definition of
\ter
this implies that there exists a
\xi
-filling
(X,u_{X})
of
(\partial V,u_{\partial V})
. Now form a closed
(B\pi,w_{1}^{\pi})
-manifold
(M,u_{M}):=(V,u_{V}^{\pi})\cup_{(\partial V,u_{\partial V})}(X,u_{X})
. By construction
c
is a representative for
\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(M,u_{M}^{\pi})
and so
\delta_{3}((u_{M}^{\pi})_{*}[M])=\smash{{\mkern 0.75mu\raisebox{-6.54443pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-5.8mu\mu}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\displaystyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\textstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptstyle J$}}{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}}(c)=x
and hence
x\in\im\delta_{3}
.
∎

An immediate consequence of
Theorem

6.10
and
Proposition

6.11
is

Corollary 6.12
.

For
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
a normal 1-type we have that for any
\xi
-3-manifold
(Y,u_{Y})
such that
\pri(Y,u_{Y})=0=\secondary(Y,u_{Y})
we have

\terG(Y,u_{Y})=\ter(Y,u_{Y})\in H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(d_{2},d_{3})=H_{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)/\im(\delta_{2},\delta_{3}).

Finally we remark that the above does
not
prove that
d_{3}
and
\delta_{3}
agree as maps, which we have not been able to determine.

Appendix A
The Serre exact sequence

Let
X
be any connected space with a finite
4
-skeleton, let
\pi=\pi_{1}(X)
and let
u_{X}^{\pi}\colon X\to B\pi
be any classifying map. Then there is the following well-known exact sequence:

(A.1)

H_{3}(X;R)\xrightarrow{(u_{X}^{\pi})_{*}}H_{3}(\pi;R)\xrightarrow{\mathfrak{f}^{X}:=d_{3}}\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R\xrightarrow{h^{\Lambda}_{R}}H_{2}(X;R)\xrightarrow{(u_{X}^{\pi})_{*}}H_{2}(\pi;R).

with coefficients in any abelian group, though throughout this section we assume that
R
is
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}/2
. This exact sequences arises as the five-term sequence of the Serre spectral sequence of the fibration
\widetilde{X}\to X\to B\pi
. The map
\mathfrak{f}^{X}
is then given by a certain third differential
d_{3}
. In the following section we prove that this map can be expressed in terms of the first
k
invariant of
X
. We do not claim originality of this result, but we have been unable to find it in the literature.

Let
k
be the first
k
-invariant of
X
, i.e let
k\in H^{3}(\pi;\pi_{2}(X))
be the element which classifies the principal fibration

B^{2}\pi_{2}(X)\to P_{2}(X)\to B\pi,

where
P_{2}(X)
is the second Postnikov stage of
X
. Let
\Lambda\to R
be the augmentation map, making
R
into a left
\Lambda
-module. Let
k^{r}\in H^{3}(\pi;\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R)
be the reduced
k
invariant and
\ev(k^{r})\in\Hom(H_{3}(\pi),R)
the evaluation map. Then

Theorem A.1
.

Let
X
be a space,
\pi=\pi_{1}(X)
and
\widetilde{X}\to X\to B\pi
be a fibration. Then for the map
d_{3}
in the exact sequence (
A.1
) we have
d_{3}=\ev(k^{r})
.

Remark A.2
.

Theorem

A.1
is a special case of a more general theorem which will be stated in the thesis of the third-named author
[
30
]
.

Proof of
Theorem

A.1
.

For simplicity denote
A=\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R
. There is the following zig-zag of fibrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{2}\pi_{2}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}^{r}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}

where the leftmost fibration is the principal fibration given by the reduced
k
-invariant
k^{r}\in H^{3}(\pi;A)
. Consider the third differential of each of the following fibrations. Since we have isomorphisms
H_{0}(\pi;H_{2}(A))\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{0}(\pi;H_{2}(B^{2}\pi_{2}(X)))\xleftarrow{\cong}H_{0}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{X}))\cong A
the zig-zag above shows that the third differentials of the above fibrations are equal.

Next we consider only the left-most fibration. It has the trivial action of
\pi
on the
\pi_{2}
of the fibre and so it can be delooped into a fibration

P_{2}^{r}(X)\to B\pi\xrightarrow{k^{r}}B^{3}A.

In particular the above composition is null-homotopic, and hence the following

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}^{r}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{2}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces EB^{3}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{3}A,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{r}}

is a map of fibrations, where
EB^{3}\simeq*
and
B^{2}A\to EB^{2}A\to B^{3}A
is the universal fibration. Hence, by naturality of the differentials of the Serre spectral sequence, we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(B\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev(k^{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}

and so
\ev(k^{r})=d_{3}
.
∎

In the present article, we need various equivalent definitions of
\ev(k^{r})
. We now define maps
\mathfrak{f}_{1},\mathfrak{f}_{2},\mathfrak{f}_{2}\colon H_{3}(B\pi)\to\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}\mathbb{Z}
and show that they are equal to
\ev(k^{r})
.

Definition A.3

(
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
)
.

Consider the following two
\Lambda
-resolutions of
\mathbb{Z}
, the lower of which is a free resolution.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}(\widetilde{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}(\widetilde{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(\widetilde{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{4}(\widetilde{B\pi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{3}(\widetilde{B\pi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}(\widetilde{B\pi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}(\widetilde{B\pi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(\widetilde{B\pi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{\widetilde{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}_{1}^{\text{chain}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}

By the fundamental theorem of homological algebra we can lift the identity map
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
to get the vertical maps. The map
\mathfrak{f}_{1}^{\text{chain}}
gives an element in the cohomology group
H^{3}(\pi;\pi_{2}(X))
. We define
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
as the image of
\mathfrak{f}^{\text{chain}}_{1}
under the following composition

H^{3}(\pi;\pi_{2}(X))\to H^{3}(\pi;\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R)\to\Hom(H_{3}(B\pi),\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R).

Definition A.4

(
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
)
.

Fix a representative of
B\pi
to be
X
with cells of dimension 3,4, …attached. Define
\mathfrak{f}_{2}\colon H_{3}(\pi;R)\to\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R
by sending
\sum_{i}r_{i}e_{3}^{i}
to
\sum_{i}([\partial e_{3}^{i}])\otimes r_{i})\in\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R
, where
\partial e_{3}^{i}
can be viewed as an element in
\pi_{2}(X)
after choosing a whisker to the basepoint. Since we land in
\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R
, the value of
\mathfrak{f}_{2}(\sum_{i}r_{i}e_{3}^{i})
does not depend on these choices.

Definition A.5

(
\mathfrak{f}_{3}
)
.

Fix a representative of
B\pi
to be
X
with cells of dimension
3,4,\dots
attached. Define a map
\mathfrak{f}_{3}\colon H_{3}(\pi;R)\to\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R
as follows. Take a class
z\in H_{3}(\pi;R)
. Thom showed
[
28
]
that every integral class up to dimension
6
can be represented by a map from an
R
-oriented manifold. Represent
z
by a
3
-dimensional manifold
z\colon W_{z}\to B\pi
. Pick some triangulation of
W_{z}
and denote
W^{\circ}_{z}
to be
W_{z}
with the interior of any
3
-simplex removed. Construct a CW-complex
C_{z}
with only
0,1,2
-cells with a homotopy equivalence to
h\colon C_{z}\to W_{z}^{\circ}
. Homotope the map
z\circ h\colon C_{z}\to B\pi
to land in
X
. Pick a homotopy inverse
h^{-1}:W_{z}^{\circ}\to C_{z}
a and obtain a map
W_{z}^{\circ}\to B\pi
, homotopic to
z|_{W_{z}^{\circ}}
, which lands in
X
. Define
\mathfrak{f}_{3}
by sending
z
to
[\partial W_{z}^{\circ}]\in\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R
.

Proposition A.6
.

We have equalities
\ev(k^{r})=\mathfrak{f}_{1}=\mathfrak{f}_{2}=\mathfrak{f}_{3}
and in particular the maps
\mathfrak{f}_{1},\mathfrak{f}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{f}_{3}
are well-defined.

Proof.

The equivalence
\ev(k^{r})=\mathfrak{f}_{1}
is by
[
6
]
, which even equates the cohomology classes in
H^{3}(\pi;\pi_{2}(X))
used in the respective definitions. For a modern treatment see
[
4
]
. Thus
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
is well-defined.

Next we establish
\mathfrak{f}_{2}=\mathfrak{f}_{1}
. The definition of
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
does not depend on the model of
B\pi
, so choose a model for the definition of
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
. Define a map
\mathfrak{f}^{\text{cell}}\colon C_{3}(\widetilde{B\pi})\to\pi_{2}(X)
by linearly extending the map defined on a
3
-cell
e_{3}
by taking the attaching map
\partial e_{3}
, which lands in
\widetilde{X}
. The map
\mathfrak{f}^{\text{cell}}
fits into the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\text{cell}}_{2}(\widetilde{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{4}^{\text{cell}}(\widetilde{B\pi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\text{cell}}_{3}(\widetilde{B\pi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\text{cell}}_{2}(\widetilde{B\pi}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{\widetilde{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}^{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}^{\text{cell}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{\pi}}

and therefore defines a cohomology class
[\mathfrak{f}^{\text{cell}}]\in H^{3}(\pi;\pi_{2}(X))
. Since
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
is well-defined, the map
\mathfrak{f}^{\text{cell}}
could have been used as
\mathfrak{f}^{\text{chain}}_{1}
in
Definition

A.3
and so
\mathfrak{f}^{\text{cell}}
maps to
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
under the composition

H^{3}(\pi;\pi_{2}(X))\to H^{3}(\pi;\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R)\to\Hom(H_{3}(B\pi),\pi_{2}(X)\otimes_{\Lambda}R).

By construction
\mathfrak{f}
also maps to
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
showing
\mathfrak{f}_{1}=\mathfrak{f}_{2}
.

Finally we establish that
\mathfrak{f}_{2}=\mathfrak{f}_{3}
. Let
B\pi
be given as above. Let
z\in H_{3}(\pi;R)
be any element represented by a map from an
R
-oriented
3
-manifold
z\colon W_{z}\to B\pi
. For any given triangulation of
W_{z}
approximate
z
by a cellular map such that all but a single
3
-cell map to
X
. The
R
-fundamental class
[W_{z}]
is given by the sum of all
3
-cells in
W_{z}
and it follows that
z
can be represented by an
R
-linear combination of
3
-cells all but one of which lie in
X
. It is now immediate that
\mathfrak{f}_{2}(z)=\mathfrak{f}_{3}(z)
since all boundaries of
3
-cells in
X
are nullhomotopic.

∎

Appendix B
James spectral sequence

Defined in the second-named author’s thesis, the James spectral sequence (henceforth JSS)
[
27
, Theorem 3.1.1]
is used to compute the bordism groups
\Omega_{n}^{\xi}
when
w_{1}(\xi)=0
. In the present paper we work with potentially non-orientable
\xi
-structures and so it is desirable to have a more general JSS. The following construction is a slight generalisation of the construction given in loc. cit.

Remark B.1
.

Although in this paper we work entirely in the smooth category (see
Remark

1.4
) the James spectral sequence as we present it also applies in the topological category and has uses there, for example when working with the 2-type of a topological 4-manifold (like in
[
24
]
). To obtain the topological version of the discussion below, replace all stable vector bundles with stable
\Top
bundles. There are appropriate versions of Thom spaces/spectra for
\Top
bundles (see
[
25
, IV 5.12]
) and these should be substituted in also. All of the below arguments work verbatim with these changes made.

Definition B.2
.

Let
\xi_{n}:B\rightarrow BO_{n}
be an
n
-dimensional vector bundle. Then we define the
Thom space
of
\xi_{n}
to be the space
\Th(\xi_{n}):=D\xi_{n}/S\xi_{n}
, where
D\xi_{n}
resp.
S\xi_{n}
are the total spaces of the disc resp. sphere bundle over
B
given by
\xi_{n}
.

By spectra, we mean sequential spectra, also known as prespectra. A sequential spectum is a sequence of spaces
E_{1},E_{2},\dots
indexed by
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and bonding maps
\Sigma E_{n}\to E_{n+1}
. For the following definition of Thom spectra see for example

[
19
, Section 2.1.4]
.

Definition B.3
.

Let
\xi\colon B\to BO
be a stable vector bundle. Define the
Thom spectrum of
\xi
as

M\xi:=(\Th(\xi_{n}),\Sigma\Th(\xi_{n})\to\Th(\xi_{n+1}))

where
\xi_{n}
is given by the homotopy pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BO_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BO}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

and the bonding map
\Sigma\Th(\xi_{n})\to\Th(\xi_{n+1}))
is the usual map given by the homotopy equivalence
\Th(\xi_{N}\oplus\varepsilon_{1})\cong\Sigma\Th(\xi_{n})
and the map
\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon_{1}\to\xi_{n+1}
over
B_{n}\to B_{n+1}
, where
\varepsilon_{1}
is the trivial
1
-dimensional vector bundle.
5
5

5

Note that the spectrum
MO
arises in this way by taking
\xi=\Id\colon BO\to BO
.

Let
F\to B\xrightarrow{f}K
be a fibration, and let
\xi\colon B\to BO
be a stable vector bundle. We choose a representative of the map
BO_{n}\to BO
such that it is a fibration which we denote by
BO_{n}\xrightarrow{\text{fib.}}BO
. It now follows that
B_{n}\xrightarrow{\text{fib.}}B
is a fibration. Let
\xi_{n}:B_{n}\to BO_{n}
be the pullback. Define
F_{n}
to be the fibre of the composition
B_{n}\xrightarrow{\text{fib.}}B\xrightarrow{\text{fib.}}K
.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\zeta_{k}
be a
k
-dimensional vector bundle over
B_{n}
. Then the composition
D(\zeta_{k})\to B_{n}\to K
is a fibration with fibre
D(\zeta_{k}|_{F_{n}})
and the following is a relative fibration

(B.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D(\zeta_{k}|_{F_{n}}),S(\zeta_{k}|_{F_{n}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D(\zeta_{k}),S(\zeta_{k}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K.}
fib.

Proof.

The first claim follows from the following commutative diagram, where the leftmost square is a pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\zeta_{k}|_{F_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\zeta_{k})}
fib.
fib.
fib.
fib.
fib.

and from the fact that a composition of fibrations is itself a fibration. The second fact follows since for any vector bundle the inclusion of the sphere bundle into the disc bundle is a cofibration.
∎

For the remainder of the section let
h
be a generalised homology theory with
\pi_{i}(h)=0
for all
i<0
.

Definition B.5
.

Define
\rho_{n}\colon\pi_{1}(K)\to\Aut(h_{*}(Th(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}})))
to be the monodromy (on the level of
h
-homology) of the relative fibration of
Lemma

B.4
for
\zeta_{k}:=\xi_{n}
. Similarly, define
\rho_{n}^{+}\colon\pi_{1}(K)\to\Aut(h_{*}(\Th(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon))
to be the monodromy corresponding to the fibration of
Lemma

B.4
for
\zeta_{k}:=\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon
.

Lemma B.6
.

There is a natural stabilisation map
g_{n}\colon\Th(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}})\to\Th(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon|_{F_{n+1}})
on Thom spaces which fits into the following commutative diagram for any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(K)
.

(B.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*}\Th(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*+1}\Th(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon|_{F_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*+1}\Th(\xi_{n+1}|_{F_{n+1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*}\Th(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*+1}\Th(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon|_{F_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{*+1}\Th(\xi_{n+1}|_{F_{n+1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n}(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{n})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{+}_{n}(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n+1}(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{n})_{*}}

Furthermore, there exists a limit of the maps
\rho_{n}
from
Definition

B.5
giving a map

\hat{\rho}\colon\pi_{1}(K)\to Aut(h_{*}M(\xi|_{F})).

Proof.

First we construct the map
g_{n}
. In the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
fib.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
fib.

we see that the map
F_{n}\to B_{n}\to B_{n+1}\to K
is null-homotopic and so there exists a map
g_{n}
fitting into the diagram as above.

We get an induced a map between two fibrations of (
B.1
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon|_{F_{n}}),S(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon|_{F_{n}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon),S(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D(\xi_{n+1}|F_{n+1}),S(\xi_{n+1}|_{F_{n+1}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D(\xi_{n+1}),S(\xi_{n+1}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{n})_{*}}

This in turn induces a map between the corresponding Thom spaces as claimed and shows that the right square of the diagram (
B.2
) commutes. The left square of (
B.2
) commutes since, by construction,
\rho^{+}_{n}
acts trivially the
\varepsilon
-summand of the vector bundle. This concludes the proof of the commutativity of (
B.2
). The outer rectangle of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BO}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BO_{n}}

is a pullback. This can be seen since the right square is a pullback and the left square is a pullback as can be shown using the universal property, using that
F
and
F_{n}
respectively are fibres of
B\xrightarrow{\text{fib.}}K
and
B_{n}\xrightarrow{\text{fib.}}K
.

Let
M\xi|_{F}
be the Thom spectrum of
\xi|_{F}
, i.e.
M\xi|_{F}
is the sequential spectrum

(\Th(\xi_{n}|_{F}),\Sigma\Th(\xi_{n}|_{F})\to\Th(\xi_{n+1}|_{F}))

with bonding maps given by
\Sigma\Th(\xi_{n}|_{F})\xrightarrow{s_{n},\simeq}\Th(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon|_{F})\xrightarrow{g_{n}}\Th(\xi_{n+1}|_{F})
. In particular, by definition of homology of a spectrum, we have the following colimit

\colim_{n\to\infty}h_{n+k}\Th\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}}=h_{k}(M\xi|_{F}).

We use this to show that the limit of maps
\rho_{n}(\gamma)
exists for
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(K)
. The above colimit can be expressed as the quotient
\sqcup h_{n+k}\Th(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}})/\sim
, where
\sim
is defined by identifying those elements that eventually share an image. This way we can define

\hat{\rho}(\gamma)\in\Aut(h_{*}M(\xi|_{F})),

for any element
x\in h_{*}M(\xi|_{F})
by choosing a preimage
x_{n}\in h_{n+k}\Th(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}})
and defining
\hat{\rho}(\gamma)(x)
to be the image of
\rho_{n}(\gamma)(x_{n})
.
∎

Theorem B.7

(James spectral sequence)
.

Let
h
be a generalised homology theory with
\pi_{i}(h)=0
for all
i<0
, let

F\to B\xrightarrow{f}K

be a fibration, and let
\xi\colon B\to BO
be a stable vector bundle. Denote by
\hat{\rho}\colon\pi_{1}(K)\to\Aut(h_{*}(M(\xi|_{F})))
the induced monodromy from
Lemma

B.6
. Then there exists a spectral sequence

E^{2}_{p,q}\cong H_{p}(K;h_{q}(M(\xi|F))_{\hat{\rho}})\,\implies\,h_{p+q}(M\xi).

Proof.

Let
F_{n}\to B_{n}\xrightarrow{f}K
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
be the sequence of fibrations determined by the standard inclusions
BO_{n}\to BO
, where
F_{n}
is defined to be the fibre (as above, we arrange
BO_{n}\to BO
to be fibrations). This yields a sequence of relative fibrations (
B.1
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}}),S(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D(\xi_{n}),S(\xi_{n}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K.}
fib.

with monodromies
\rho_{n}
. Accordingly there is a corresponding sequence of relative, twisted Serre spectral sequences (see
[
5
, Theorem 9.34]
)

\prescript{n}{}{E}^{2}_{p,q}\cong H_{p}(K;h^{n}_{q}(D(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}}),S(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}}))_{\rho_{n}})\implies h^{n}_{p+q}(D(\xi_{n}),S(\xi_{n}))\cong h_{p+q}(M\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}}),

with differentials denoted by
\prescript{n}{}{d}^{k}_{p,q}
. The last isomorphism above comes from the Thom-isomorphism theorem, and we have used the homology theory
h^{n}
defined by
h_{*}^{n}=h_{*+n}
.
6
6

6

Note that there is an oversight in the argument in
[
27
]
at this point: it is not known if the monodromies
\rho_{n}
are trivial even if the monodromy
\hat{\rho}
is. Our argument fixes this by always allowing our Serre spectral sequences to be twisted.

We now wish to obtain the JSS as a direct limit of these relative, twisted Serre spectral sequences. Recall the composition of maps

h_{*}\Th(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}})\xrightarrow{s_{n}}h_{*+1}\Th(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon|_{F_{n}})\xrightarrow{(g_{n})_{*}}h_{*+1}\Th(\xi_{n+1}|_{F_{n+1}})

where
s_{n}
is the suspension isomorphism and
(g_{n})_{*}
was defined in
Lemma

B.6
.

Using the same lemma, this composition induces a homomorphism of spectral sequences, which we denote by
\prescript{n}{}{\varphi}^{k}_{p,q}
. For example on the second page
\prescript{n}{}{\varphi}^{2}_{p,q}
is given by the following composition

\displaystyle\prescript{n}{}{E}^{2}_{p,q}

\displaystyle\cong H_{p}(K;h^{n}_{q}(D(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}}),S(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}}))_{\rho_{n}})

\displaystyle\cong H_{p}(K;\widetilde{h}^{n}_{q}(\Th(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}}))_{\rho_{n}})\xrightarrow{(s_{n})_{*},\cong}H_{p}(K;\widetilde{h}^{n}_{q+1}(\Th(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon|_{F_{n}}))_{\rho^{+}_{n}})

\displaystyle\cong H_{p}(K;\widetilde{h}^{n+1}_{q}(\Th(\xi_{n}\oplus\varepsilon|_{F_{n}}))_{\rho^{+}_{n}})\xrightarrow{(g_{n})_{*}}H_{p}(K;\widetilde{h}^{n+1}_{q}(\Th(\xi_{n+1}|_{F_{n+1}}))_{\rho_{n+1}})\cong\prescript{n+1}{}{E}^{2}_{p,q}

where the first isomorphism of the third line is given by reindexing
h_{q}^{1}=h_{q+1}
. By the naturality of the Serre spectral sequence we obtain a commutative diagram involving the differentials

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{n}{}{E}^{k}_{p,q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{n}{}{E}^{k}_{p-k,q+k-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{n+1}{}{E}^{k}_{p,q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{n+1}{}{E}^{k}_{p-k,q+k-1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{n}{}{d}^{k}_{p,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{n}{}{\varphi}^{k}_{p,q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{n}{}{\varphi}^{k}_{p-k,q+k-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{n+1}{}{d}^{k}_{p,q}}

Since direct limit is an exact functor the colimit

(E^{k}_{p,q},d^{k}_{p,q}):=\colim_{n\to\infty}(\prescript{n}{}{E}^{k}_{p,q},d^{k}_{p,q}).

preserves differentials and gives that our colimit indeed defines a spectral sequence.

The calculation of the second page is exactly as in
[
27
]
and we obtain

E^{2}_{p,q}=\colim_{n\to\infty}H_{p}(K;h_{q}(T(\xi_{n}|_{F_{n}}))_{\rho_{n}})\cong H_{p}(K;h_{q}(M\xi|_{F})_{\hat{\rho}}).\qed

In all applications we will take
h=\pi^{s}
(i.e. stable homotopy theory), the stable vector bundle
\xi=\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
will be a normal 1-type and we will take as input the fibration

(B.3)

\BSpin\to B\to B\pi,

that gives our normal
1
-type.

We now express the monodromy
\hat{\rho}
.

Lemma B.8
.

Applying the JSS to the fibration (
B.3
) using
h=\pi^{s}
yields a monodromy
\hat{\rho}\colon\pi_{1}(K)\to\Aut(\Omega^{\Spin}_{i})
given in the low dimensions by

\begin{cases}\hat{\rho}(\gamma)=\Id&\text{for }i=1,2,3\\
\hat{\rho}(\gamma)=w_{1}(\gamma)&\text{for }i=0,4.\end{cases}

Proof.

Any
\mathbb{Z}/2
-action on
\mathbb{Z}/2\cong\Omega_{1}^{\Spin}\cong\Omega_{2}^{\Spin}
is trivial.

For
i=0,4
compare the fibration
B\Spin\to B\to K
to the fibration
BSO\to BO\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)
. This gives an isomorphism of the coefficients
\Omega_{i}^{\Spin}\to\Omega^{SO}_{i}
for
i=0,4
. The monodromy on
BSO\to BO\to K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)
is given by the orientation reversal. It follows from considering the signature that orientation reversal gives the nontrivial involution on
\Omega^{SO}_{0}
and
\Omega_{4}^{SO}
and naturality concludes the proof.
∎

Applying the JSS to this data gives

E^{2}_{p,q}\cong H_{p}(\pi;\pi^{s}_{q}(M(\xi|\BSpin))_{\hat{\rho}})\cong H_{p}(\pi;(\Omega^{\Spin}_{q})_{w_{1}^{\pi}})\ \implies\ \pi^{s}_{p+q}(M\xi)\cong\Omega^{\xi}_{p+q}.

B.1.
The second differential of the JSS

We now work to prove the following result concerning the second differential
d_{2}
in the JSS for the 1-type
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
. In what follows the generalised homology theory
h
used for any JSS is always assumed to be stable homotopy, i.e.
h=\pi^{s}
.

Proposition B.9

(cf.
[
27
, Theorem 3.1.3]
,
[
24
, Proposition 5.9]
)
.

The second differential
d_{2}
in the JSS for the 1-type
\xi(\pi,w_{1}^{\pi},w_{2}^{\pi})
is given as

d_{2}=(\Sq^{2}(-)+\Sq^{1}(-)\smile w_{1}+-\smile w_{2})^{*}\colon H_{r}(\pi;\Omega_{1}^{\Spin})\to H_{r-2}(\pi;\Omega_{2}^{\Spin})

for
r\leq 4
and

d_{2}=(\Sq^{2}(-)+\Sq^{1}(-)\smile w_{1}+-\smile w_{2})^{*}\circ\red_{2}\colon H_{r}(\pi;\Omega_{0}^{\Spin})\to H_{r-2}(\pi;\Omega_{1}^{\Spin})

for
r\leq 5
.

To prove this we will follow Orson-Powell
[
24
, Section 5]
7
7

7

A notational difference is that we will write
\xi
for vector bundles whereas Orson-Powell write
v
.
where they proved a similar result (cited above), expanding on the work of the second-named author also cited above. The extra details will make it easier to pinpoint the alterations necessary for the non-orientable case.

Lemma B.10

(Non-orientable version of
[
24
, Lemma 5.3]
)
.

Let
\xi\colon B\to BO
be a (potentially non-orientable) stable vector bundle over a CW complex. Then

\Sq^{2}\phi^{\infty}(-)=\phi^{\infty}(\Sq^{2}(-)+w_{2}(\xi)\smile-+w_{1}(\xi)\smile\Sq^{1}(-)),

for
\phi^{\infty}
the stable Thom isomorphism map.

Proof.

The proof proceeds exactly as in
[
24
, Proof of Lemma 5.3]
except instead of the term (in their notation)
U_{n}\smile\rho^{*}_{n}(w_{1}(\xi_{n}))\smile\Sq^{1}(\rho_{n}^{*}(x))
vanishing this now gives precisely the additional term
\Sq^{1}(-)\smile w_{1}(\xi)
.
∎

To ease notation further we will now denote by

\Sq^{2}_{w_{1}(\xi),w_{2}(\xi)}(-):=\Sq^{2}(-)+w_{2}(\xi)\smile-+w_{1}(\xi)\smile\Sq^{1}(-).

The next step is compute the differentials for a certain degenerate version of the JSS, which we state and prove now.

Lemma B.11

(Non-orientable version of
[
24
, Lemma 5.4]
)
.

Let
B
be a CW-complex and consider the input for the James spectral sequence

\displaystyle*\to B\xrightarrow{\id}B

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\xi\colon B\to BO

Then the James spectral sequence has the following differentials
d_{2}^{r,s}\colon E_{2}^{r,s}\to E_{2}^{r-2,s+1}
for all
r\geq 2
.

(1)

Via the universal coefficient theorem,
d^{r,1}_{2}\colon H_{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{r-2}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is identified
8
8

8

In this section only the notation
{(-)}^{\vee}
denotes the functor
\Hom(-;\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
with
(\Sq^{2}_{w_{1}(\xi),w_{2}(\xi)})^{\vee}
. More precisely, the following square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{r-2}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{r-2}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\vee}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{r,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Sq^{2}_{w_{1}(\xi),w_{2}(\xi)})^{\vee}}

(2)

The map
d_{2}^{r,0}\colon H_{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})\to H_{r-2}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is identified with reduction mod 2 followed by
(\Sq^{2}_{w_{1}(\xi),w_{2}(\xi)})^{\vee}
. More precisely, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{r-2}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{r-2}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\vee}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{r,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{red}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Sq^{2}_{w_{1}(\xi),w_{2}(\xi)})^{\vee}}

Proof.

We follow
[
24
, Proof of Lemma 5.4]
. First note that
\phi_{\infty}
still induces a natural isomorphism between the JSS for the degenerate fibration listed above and the Atiyah-Hirzebruch spectral sequence for the Thom spectrum
M(\xi)
. In the latter the differentials are well-known to be given as

\displaystyle d_{2}^{r,1}=(\Sq^{2}_{w_{1}(\xi),w_{2}(\xi)})^{\vee}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad d_{2}^{r,0}=(\Sq^{2}_{w_{1}(\xi),w_{2}(\xi)})^{\vee}\circ\red_{2}.

The proof then collapses to showing that a large diagram commutes, which is exactly the same diagram as in
[
24
, Page 235, Diagram (5)]
aside from the third square, which is replaced by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{r}(M(\xi);\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{r-2}(M(\xi);\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{r-2}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Sq^{2})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi^{\infty})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Sq^{2}_{w_{1}(\xi),w_{2}(\xi)})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\phi^{\infty})^{\vee}}

and this square commutes by
B.10
; and the first square, which is replaced by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{r}(M(\xi);\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{r-2}(M(\xi);\mathbb{Z}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{r-2}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\red_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\red_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\infty}}

which also clearly commutes. All of the other squares commute by the arguments in
[
24
]
unchanged.
∎

With the previous lemmas in hand, we can state and prove a general statement which says that, in situations where the differential can be compared to the degenerate case of the JSS, we can also compute the differential.

Proposition B.12

(Non-orientable version of
[
24
, Corollary 5.7]
)
.

Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BO}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)\times K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

be a pullback square of fibrations. Suppose there exist classes
w_{1}\in H^{1}(K;\mathbb{Z}/2)
and
w_{2}\in H^{2}(K;\mathbb{Z}/2)
such that
f^{*}(w_{1})=w_{1}(\xi)
and
f^{*}(w_{2})=w_{2}(\xi)
.

(1)

Suppose for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
that
f_{*}\colon H_{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{r}(K;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is surjective. Then, in the sense of
Lemma

B.11
,
d^{r,1}_{2}\colon H_{r}(K;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{r-2}(K;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is
(\Sq^{2}_{w_{1},w_{2}})^{\vee}
.

(2)

Suppose for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
that
f_{*}\colon H_{r}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}(\xi)})\to H_{r}(K;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}})
is surjective. Then, in the sense of
Lemma

B.11
,
d_{2}^{r,0}\colon H_{r}(K;\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}})\to H_{r-2}(K;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is reduction mod 2 followed by
(\Sq^{2}_{w_{1},w_{2}})^{\vee}
.

Note that we have a stronger requirement for the fibration in
B.12
than in
[
24
, Corollary 5.7]
, because we need the twisting in the JSS for
*\to B\to B
to agree with the twisting in our fibration
F\to B\to K
. With this extra assumption it follows from
B.8
that this is the case, and hence the domain of the differential
d_{0}^{r,0}
is correct as written.

Proof.

The additional assumption of
[
24
, Corollary 5.7]
is satisfied, since the map
*\to B\Spin\to BO
induces an isomorphism on the Thom spectra
\pi_{i}(M(*))\cong\pi_{i}^{st}\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{i}(M(\xi\circ i))
for
i=0,1,2
. The proof of (1) is entirely analogous to that in loc. cit.; the proof of (2) is left to the reader.

We give the setup. By assumption, for any
x\in H_{r}(K;\mathbb{Z}/2)
there exists a
y\in H_{r}(B;Z/2)
such that
f_{*}(y)=x
. The proof then proceeds exactly as in
[
24
]
except for differences in their final calculation, which we now redo. Let
u\in H^{r-2}(K;\mathbb{Z}/2)
be arbitrary. We then have that

\displaystyle\left((f^{*})^{\vee}\circ\Sq^{2}_{w_{1}(\xi),w_{2}(\xi)}(u)\right)

\displaystyle(y)=\left(\Sq^{2}_{w_{1}(\xi),w_{2}(\xi)}(f^{*}u)\right)(y)

\displaystyle=\Sq^{2}(f^{*}u)(y)+\left(w_{2}(\xi)\cup(f^{*}u)\right)(y)+\left(w_{1}(v)\cup\Sq^{1}(f^{*}(u))\right)(y)

\displaystyle=\Sq^{2}(u)(f_{*}y)+\left((f^{*}w_{2})\cup(f^{*}u)\right)(y)+\left(f^{*}w_{1}\cup f^{*}\Sq^{1}(u)\right)(y)

\displaystyle=\Sq^{2}(u)(f_{*}y)+\left(w_{2}\cup u\right)(f_{*}y)+\left(w_{1}\cup\Sq^{1}(u)\right)(f_{*}y)

\displaystyle=\Sq^{2}(u)(x)+\left(w_{2}\cup u+w_{1}\cup\Sq^{1}(u)\right)(x)

\displaystyle=\Sq^{2}_{w_{1},w_{2}}(x).\qed

We now finish this section by using
B.12
to prove
Proposition

B.9
, which was stated earlier.

Proof of
Proposition

B.9
.

In the proof of
[
27
, Theorem 3.1.3]
the second-named author shows that
H_{i}(B;\mathbb{Z}/2)\to H_{i}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is surjective for
i\leq 4
and
H_{i}(B;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{i}(\pi;\mathbb{Z})
is surjective for
i\leq 5
. In the non-orientable case the proof is the same and uses that
H_{5}(K(\mathbb{Z}/2,1)\times K(\mathbb{Z}/2,2);\mathbb{Z}^{w_{1}})
is finite. We conclude the proof using
B.12
.
∎
